(\iota_{P,i})_{i<\lambda}
,
(\alpha_{P,i})_{i<\lambda}
,
and
(\beta_{P,i})_{i<1\dotplus\lambda}
with

\bullet

u_{P,0}=\tau_{P,0}
and
\psi_{P,0}=0
;

\bullet

\tau_{P,i}\in\operatorname{term}\psi_{P,i}
or
\tau_{P,i}\in\operatorname{term}u_{P,i}
for all
i<\lambda
;

\bullet

\tau_{P,i}\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}\cup\{\omega\}\Longrightarrow\lambda=i+1
for all
i<\lambda
;

\bullet

For
i<\lambda
, the hyperserial expansion of
\mathfrak{m}_{P,i}

is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{P,i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{P,i+1}}(L_{\beta_{P,i}}E_{\alpha_{P,i}}^{u_{P,i+1}})^{\iota_{P,i}}.

We call
\lambda
the
length
of
P
and we write
|P|:=\lambda
. We say that
P
is
infinite
if
|P|=\omega
and
finite
otherwise. We set
a_{P,0}:=a
. For
0<i<|P|
, we define

\displaystyle(s_{P,i},a_{P,i})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(-1,\psi_{P,i})&\text{{\hskip 0.0pt}if $\mathfrak{m}_{P,i}\in\operatorname{supp}\psi_{P,i}$}\\
(1,u_{P,i})&\text{{\hskip 0.0pt}if $\mathfrak{m}_{P,i}\in\operatorname{supp}u_{P,i}$}.\end{array}\right.

By Lemma
5.8
, those cases are mutually exclusive so

(s_{P,i},a_{P,i})
is well-defined. For
a\in\mathbf{No}
, we say that

P
is a path in
a
if
P(0)\in\operatorname{term}a
.

For
k\leqslant|P|
, we let
P_{\nearrow k}
denote the path of length
|P|-k
in
a_{P,k}
with

\forall i<|P|-k,\tau_{P_{\nearrow k},i}:=\tau_{P,k+i}.

Example 5.9

Let us find all the paths in the monomial
\mathfrak{m}
of
Example
5.3
. We have a representation
(
28
) of
\mathfrak{m}
as a hyperseries

\mathfrak{m}=\mathrm{e}^{2E_{\omega^{2}}^{L_{\omega^{\small{2}}}\omega+1}-E_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}L_{1}\omega}}(L_{\omega}\omega)

which by Lemma
5.7
is unique. There are nine
paths in
\mathfrak{m}
, namely

\bullet

1 path
(\mathfrak{m})
of length
1
;

\bullet

3 paths
\left(\mathfrak{m},2E_{\omega^{2}}^{L_{\omega^{\small{2}}}\omega+1}\right)
,
\left(\mathfrak{m},-E_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}L_{1}\omega}\right)
, and
(\mathfrak{m},\omega)
of length
2
;

\bullet

3 paths
\left(\mathfrak{m},2E_{\omega^{2}}^{L_{\omega^{\small{2}}}\omega+1},L_{\omega^{2}}\omega\right)
,
\left(\mathfrak{m},2E_{\omega^{2}}^{L_{\omega^{\small{2}}}\omega+1},1\right)
,
\left(\!\mathfrak{m},-E_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}L_{1}\omega},\frac{1}{2}L_{1}\omega\!\right)
of length
3
;

\bullet

2 paths
\left(\mathfrak{m},2E_{\omega^{2}}^{L_{\omega^{\small{2}}}\omega+1},L_{\omega^{2}}\omega,\omega\right)
and
\left(\mathfrak{m},-E_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}L_{1}\omega},\frac{1}{2}L_{1}\omega,\omega\right)
of length
4
.

Note that the paths which cannot be extended into strictly longer paths are
those whose last value is a real number or
\omega
.

Infinite paths occur in so-called nested numbers that will be studied in more
detail in Section
6
.

Definition 5.10

Let
a\in\mathbf{No}
and let
P
be a path in a. We
say that an index
i<|P|
is
bad
for
(P,a)
if one of the following conditions is satisfied

1.

\mathfrak{m}_{P,i}
is not the

\preccurlyeq
-minimum of
\operatorname{supp}u_{P,i}
;

2.

\mathfrak{m}_{P,i}=\min\operatorname{supp}u_{P,i}
and
\beta_{P,i}\neq 0
;

3.

\mathfrak{m}_{P,i}=\min\operatorname{supp}u_{P,i}
and
\beta_{P,i}=0
and
r_{P,i}\not\in\{-1,1\}
;

4.

\mathfrak{m}_{P,i}=\min\operatorname{supp}u_{P,i}
and
\beta_{P,i}=0
and
r_{P,i}\in\{-1,1\}
and

\mathfrak{m}_{P,i}\in\operatorname{supp}\psi_{P,i}
.

The index
i
is
good
for
(P,a)

if it is not bad for
(P,a)
.

If
P
is infinite, then we say that it is

good
if
(P,\tau_{P,0})
is good for all but a finite number of indices. In the opposite case,
we say that
P
is a
bad
path. An element
a\in\mathbf{No}
is said to be

well-nested
every path in

a
is good.

Remark 5.11

The above definition extends the former definitions of paths
in
[
30
,
38
,
10
]
. More precisely, a path
P
with with

\alpha_{P,i}=1
(whence
\psi_{P,i}=0
) for all
i<|P|
,
corresponds to a path for these former definitions. The validity of the
axiom
T4
for
\mathbf{No}
means that those paths are good.
With Theorem
1.1
, we will extend this result to all paths.

Lemma 5.12

For
\mathfrak{m}\in(L_{\mathbf{On}}\omega)^{\pm 1}
and for any path
P
in
\mathfrak{m}
, we have
|P|\leqslant 2
. For
a\in\mathbb{L}\circ\omega
and for any path
P
in

a
, we have
|P|\leqslant 3
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{l}\in\mathfrak{L}\setminus\{-1\}
and let
P
be
a path in
\mathfrak{l}\circ\omega
. If there is an ordinal
\gamma
with

\mathfrak{l}=\ell_{\gamma}
, then the hyperserial expansion of

\mathfrak{l}\circ\omega
is
L_{\gamma}\omega
, so
|P|=1
if

\gamma=0
and
|P|=2
otherwise. If there is an ordinal
\gamma
with

\mathfrak{l}=\ell_{\gamma}^{-1}
, then the hyperserial expansion of

\mathfrak{l}\circ\omega
is
(L_{\gamma}\omega)^{-1}
and
|P|=2
.

Assume now that
\mathfrak{l}\not\in\ell_{\mathbf{On}}^{\pm 1}
. If
\log\mathfrak{l}\circ\omega
is not tail-atomic, then hyperserial expansion of

\mathfrak{l}\circ\omega
is
\mathfrak{l}\circ\omega=\mathrm{e}^{\log\mathfrak{l}\circ\omega}
. If
\log\mathfrak{l}\circ\omega
is
tail-atomic, then the hyperserial expansion of
\mathfrak{l}\circ\omega

is
\mathfrak{l}\circ\omega=\mathrm{e}^{\psi\circ\omega}(\mathfrak{a}\circ\omega)^{\iota}
for a certain log-atomic
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbb{L}
. In both cases,
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a path in some monomial in

L_{\mathbf{On}}\omega
, whence
|P_{\nearrow 1}|\leqslant 2
and
|P|\leqslant 3
, by the previous argument.
∎

Definition 5.13

Let
P,Q
be paths. We say that
Q
is a
subpath
of

P
, or equivalently that
P

extends

Q
, if there exists a
k<|P|
with
Q=P_{\nearrow k}
. For
a\in\mathbf{No}
, we say that
Q
is a

subpath in

a
if there is a path
P
in
a
such that

Q
is a subpath of
P
. We say that
P

shares a subpath
with

a
if there is a subpath of
P
which is a subpath in
a
.

Let
P
be a finite path and let
Q
be a path with
Q(0)\in\operatorname{supp}u_{P,|P|}\cup\operatorname{supp}\psi_{P,|P|}
. Then we define
P\ast Q
to be the path
(P(0),\ldots,P(|P|),Q(0),\ldots)

of length
|P|+|Q|
.

Lemma 5.14

Let
\lambda\in\omega^{\mathbf{On}}
and

\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\lambda}
. Let
P
be a path in

\mathfrak{m}
with
|P|>1
. Then
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath in

L_{\lambda}\mathfrak{m}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.12
, we have
\mathfrak{m}\not\in(L_{\mathbf{On}}\omega)^{\pm 1}
. If
\mathfrak{m}
has a hyperserial
expansion of the form
\mathfrak{m}=\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\gamma}\omega)^{\iota}
, then
P_{\nearrow 1}
must be a path in
\psi
. So
\psi

is non-zero and thus
\lambda=1
. It follows that
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a
path in
\log\mathfrak{m}=\psi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\iota(L_{\gamma}\omega)^{\iota}
.
Otherwise, let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{u})^{\iota}
be the hyperserial expansion of
\mathfrak{m}
. If

P_{\nearrow 1}
is a path in
\psi
, then it is a path in
\log\mathfrak{m}
as above. Otherwise, it is a path in
u
. Assume that
\lambda=1
. If
\alpha=1
, then we have
\psi=0
and
\log\mathfrak{m}=\iota u
so
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a path in
\log\mathfrak{m}
. If
\alpha>1
,
then
\log\mathfrak{m}=\psi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\iota L_{\beta+1}E_{\alpha}^{u}
where

L_{\beta+1}E_{\alpha}^{u}
is a hyperserial expansion, so
P_{\nearrow 1}

is a path in
\log\mathfrak{m}
. Assume now that
\lambda>1
, so
\psi=0
,
\iota=1
, and
\alpha\geqslant\omega
. We must have
\beta\geq\!\!\!\geq\lambda_{/\omega}
so there are
\beta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{On}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
\beta^{\prime}\gg\lambda_{/\omega}
and
\beta=\beta^{\prime}+\lambda_{/\omega}n
. We have
L_{\lambda}\mathfrak{m}=L_{\beta^{\prime}+\lambda}E_{\alpha}^{u}-n
where
L_{\beta^{\prime}+\lambda}E_{\alpha}^{u}
is a
hyperserial expansion, so
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a path in
L_{\lambda}\mathfrak{m}
.
∎

Lemma 5.15

Let
a\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
,
\alpha\in\omega^{\mathbf{On}}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}^{>}
. If
P
is a path in

\sharp_{\alpha}(a)
with
|P|>2
, then
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath
in
\mathfrak{d}_{E_{\alpha k}a}
.

Proof.

We prove this by induction on
\alpha k
, for any number
a\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
. We consider
a\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
, and a
fixed path
P
in
\sharp_{\alpha}(a)
with
|P|>1
.

Assume that
\alpha=k=1
. We have
\sharp_{1}(a)=a_{\succ}
and

\mathfrak{d}_{\exp a}=\mathrm{e}^{a_{\succ}}
. Assume that
a_{\succ}=\psi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\iota\mathfrak{a}
for certain
\psi\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ}
,

\iota\in\{-1,1\}
, and
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\omega}
. Let

\mathfrak{a}=L_{\gamma}E_{\lambda}^{u}
be the hyperserial expansion of

\mathfrak{a}
. If
\lambda=\omega
, then
\gamma=0
and the hyperserial
expansion of
\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{a}}
is
\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{a}}=E_{\omega}^{u+1}
. Therefore
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath in

\mathfrak{d}_{\exp a}=\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\omega}^{u+1})^{\iota}
. If

\lambda>\omega
, then the hyperserial expansion of

\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{a}}
is
\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{a}}=L_{\gamma+1}E_{\lambda}^{u}
. Therefore
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath in

\mathfrak{d}_{\exp a}=\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\gamma+1}E_{\lambda}^{u})^{\iota}
. Finally, if
\mathrm{e}^{a_{\succ}}
is not
tail-atomic, then
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath in
\mathfrak{d}_{\exp a}=\left(E_{1}^{\smash{\epsilon a_{\succ}}}\right)^{\epsilon}
, where
\epsilon\in\{-1,1\}
is the sign of
a_{\succ}
.

Now assume that
\alpha=1
,
k>1
, and that the result holds strictly
below
k
. We have
E_{k}a=E_{k-1}(\exp a)
where
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a
subpath in
\mathfrak{d}_{\exp a}
by the previous argument. We have

r\mathfrak{d}_{\exp a}\trianglelefteqslant\sharp_{1}(\exp a)
for a
certain
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
, so
Q:=(r\mathfrak{d}_{\exp a})\ast P_{\nearrow 1}
is a path in
\sharp_{1}(\exp a)
. The induction
hypothesis on
k-1
implies that
Q_{\nearrow 1}=P_{\nearrow 1}
is a
subpath in
\mathfrak{d}_{E_{k}a}
.

Assume now that
\alpha\geqslant\omega
and that the result holds strictly
below
\alpha
. Write
v:=\sharp_{\alpha}(a)
.
By (
13
), there exist
\eta\in\mathbf{On}
,
n<\omega
, and
\delta\in\mathbf{No}
with
\beta:=\omega^{\eta}<\alpha
and

E_{\alpha}a=E_{\beta n}(L_{\beta n}E_{\alpha}^{v}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta).

Assume for contradiction that there is a
\gamma\in\mathbf{On}
with

E_{\alpha}^{v}=L_{\gamma}\omega
. We must have
\gamma\geq\!\!\!\geq\alpha_{/\omega}
, so there are a number
n\in\mathbb{N}
and an ordinal
\gamma^{\prime}\geq\!\!\!\geq\alpha
with
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}+\alpha_{/\omega}n
. We have
v={L_{\gamma^{\prime}+\alpha}\omega-n}
. By Lemma
5.12
, this
contradicts the fact that
|P|>2
. So by
Lemma
5.4
, there exist
\beta\in\omega^{\mathbf{On}}
and
\gamma\in\mathbf{On}
with
\beta\geqslant\alpha
,
\gamma\omega<\beta
,
E_{\alpha}^{v}=L_{\gamma}E_{\beta}^{u}
,
and
E_{\beta}^{u}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\beta}\setminus L_{<\beta}\mathbf{Mo}_{\beta\omega}
. Since
E_{\alpha}^{v}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha}
,
we must have
\gamma\geq\!\!\!\geq\alpha_{/\omega}
so there are a number
n\in\mathbb{N}
and an ordinal
\gamma^{\prime}\geq\!\!\!\geq\alpha
with
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}+\alpha_{/\omega}n
(note that
n=0
whenever
\alpha_{/\omega}=\alpha
). Thus
v+n=L_{\alpha}L_{\gamma^{\prime}+\alpha_{/\omega}n}E_{\beta}^{u}+n=L_{\gamma^{\prime}+\alpha}E_{\beta}^{u}
is a monomial with
hyperserial expansion
v+n=L_{\gamma^{\prime}+\alpha}E_{\beta}^{u}
. There is
no path in
n
of length
>1
, so
P
must be a path in
L_{\gamma^{\prime}+\alpha}E_{\beta}^{u}
. We deduce that
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a path in
u
.
Consequently,
Q=(L_{\gamma}E_{\beta}^{u})\ast P_{\nearrow 1}
is a path
in
E_{\alpha}^{v}
with
|Q|=|P|>1
. Applying
n
times
Lemma
5.14
, we deduce that
Q_{\nearrow 1}=P_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath in
L_{\beta n}E_{\alpha}^{v}
, hence in

\sharp_{\beta}(L_{\beta n}E_{\alpha}a)
. Consider a path
R
in

\sharp_{\beta}(L_{\beta n}E_{\alpha}a)
with
P_{\nearrow 1}=R_{\nearrow i}
for a certain
i>0
. Applying the induction hypothesis
for
L_{\beta n}E_{\alpha}a
and
\beta n
in the roles of
a
and
\alpha k
, the path
R_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath in
\mathfrak{d}_{E_{\beta n}(L_{\beta n}E_{\alpha}a)}=\mathfrak{d}_{E_{\alpha}a}
. Therefore

P_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath in
\mathfrak{d}_{E_{\alpha}a}
. We deduce as
in the case
\alpha=1
that
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath
in
\mathfrak{d}_{E_{\alpha k}a}
.
∎

Lemma 5.16

Let
\psi\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ}
, and

\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbf{Mo}^{\neq}
with
\operatorname{supp}\psi\succ\log\mathfrak{m}
. Let
P
be a path in
\mathfrak{m}
with
|P|>1
. Then

P_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath in
\mathrm{e}^{\psi}\mathfrak{m}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathrm{e}^{\varphi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{u})^{\iota}
be a
hyperserial expansion. The condition
\operatorname{supp}\psi\succ\log\mathfrak{m}
implies
\varphi+\psi=\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\psi
, whence

\mathrm{e}^{\psi}\mathfrak{m}=\mathrm{e}^{\psi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varphi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{u})^{\iota}
is also a hyperserial expansion. In particular

P_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath in
\mathrm{e}^{\psi}\mathfrak{m}
.
∎

Corollary 5.17

Let
\alpha=\omega^{\nu}\in\mathbf{On}
,

\beta\in\mathbf{On}
with
\beta<\alpha
, and
\varphi\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}
. If
P
is an infinite path, then
P
shares a
subpath with
\varphi
if and only if it shares a subpath with
L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{\varphi}
.

Proof.

Write
\beta=\omega^{\eta_{1}}m_{1}+\cdots+\omega^{\eta_{k}}m_{k}
in
Cantor normal form, with
\eta_{1}>\cdots>\eta_{k}
and
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{>}
and let

\mathfrak{a}_{i}:=L_{\omega^{\eta_{1}}m_{1}+\cdots+\omega^{\eta_{i-1}}m_{i-1}}E_{\alpha}^{\varphi}

for all
i=1,\ldots,k
.

Assume that
P
shares a subpath with
\varphi
. In other words, there is a
path
R
in
\varphi
which has a common subpath with
P
. The path
R
must
be infinite, so by
Lemma

5.15
, it shares a subpath with

E_{\alpha}^{\varphi}=\mathfrak{a}_{1}
. Let us prove by induction on
i=1,\ldots,k
that
R
shares a subpath with
E_{\alpha}^{\varphi}=\mathfrak{a}_{i}
. Assuming that this holds for
i<k
, we note that

\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is
L_{<\omega^{\eta_{i-1}}\omega}
-atomic, hence
L_{<\omega^{\eta_{i}}}
-
atomic. So
P
shares a subpath with

\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}
by
Lemma

5.14
and the induction
hypothesis. We conclude by induction that
P
shares a subpath with

\mathfrak{a}_{k}=L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{\varphi}
.

Suppose conversely that
P
shares a subpath with
L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{\varphi}=\mathfrak{a}_{k}
. By induction on
i=k-1,\ldots,1
, it follows from
Lemma

5.15
that
P
shares a subpath
with
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
. Applying
Lemma

5.14
to

\mathfrak{a}_{1}=E_{\alpha}^{\varphi}
, we conclude that
P
shares a
subpath with
\varphi
.
∎

5.3
Deconstruction lemmas

In this subsection, we list several results on the interaction between the
simplicity relation
\sqsubseteq
and various operations in
(\mathbf{No},+,\times,(L_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbf{On}})
.

Lemma 5.18

[
26
, Theorem 3.3]
For
a,b\in\mathbf{No}
, we have

a\sqsubseteq b\Longleftrightarrow\mathord{-}a\sqsubseteq\mathord{-}b.

Lemma 5.19

[
26
, Theorem 5.12(a)]
For

\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbf{Mo}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
, we have

\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathfrak{m}\sqsubseteq r\mathfrak{m}.

Lemma 5.20

[
5
, Proposition 4.20]
Let
\varphi\in\mathbf{No}
. For
\delta,\varepsilon
with
\delta,\varepsilon\prec\operatorname{supp}\varphi
, we have

\displaystyle\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta\sqsubseteq\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\delta\sqsubseteq\varepsilon.

Lemma 5.21

[
10
, Corollary 4.21]
For
\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbf{Mo}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\sqsubseteq\mathfrak{n}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}^{-1}\sqsubseteq\mathfrak{n}^{-1}.

Lemma 5.22

[
10
, Proposition 4.23]
Given
\varphi,a,b
in
\mathbf{No}_{\succ}
with
a,b\prec\operatorname{supp}\varphi
, we
have

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{a}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{e}^{b}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}a}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{e}^{\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}b}.

Lemma 5.23

[
10
, Proposition 4.24]
Given

\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbf{Mo}^{\succ}
with
\log\mathfrak{m}\prec\mathfrak{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\sqsubseteq\mathfrak{n}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{m}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{n}}.

Lemma 5.24

Let
\varphi\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
, let
\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbf{Mo}^{\succ}\cap\mathbf{No}^{\prec\operatorname{supp}\varphi}
with
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[\mathfrak{n}]
, and let
\delta\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ}
with
\delta\prec\operatorname{supp}\mathfrak{n}
. Then

\mathfrak{m}\sqsubseteq\mathfrak{n}\Longrightarrow\mathrm{e}^{\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathfrak{m}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{e}^{\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathfrak{n}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta}.

Proof.

The condition
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[\mathfrak{n}]
yields

\log\mathfrak{m}\prec\mathfrak{n}
. We have
\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{m}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{n}}
by Lemma
5.23
. The identity

\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{Mo}^{\succ}}=\mathbf{Smp}_{\mathcal{P}}
implies that

\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{m}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{e}^{|r|\mathfrak{n}}
, whence

\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathfrak{m}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{e}^{r\mathfrak{n}}

by Lemma
5.21
. Lemma
5.22
yields
\mathrm{e}^{\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathfrak{m}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{e}^{\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathfrak{n}}
. Since
\mathrm{e}^{0}=1\sqsubseteq\mathrm{e}^{\delta}\in\mathbf{No}^{>}
, we may apply Lemma
5.22

to
\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathfrak{n}
and
\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathfrak{n}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta
to obtain
\mathrm{e}^{\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathfrak{n}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{e}^{\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathfrak{n}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta}
. We conclude using the
transitivity of
\sqsubseteq
.
∎

Lemma 5.25

Let
\alpha\in\omega^{\mathbf{On}}
with
\alpha>1
. For
\varphi,\psi\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}
with
L_{\alpha}E_{<\alpha}\varphi<\psi
, we have

\varphi\sqsubseteq\psi\Longrightarrow E_{\alpha}^{\varphi}\sqsubseteq E_{\alpha}^{\psi}.

Proof.

By (
18
), we have

E_{\alpha}\varphi=\left\{E_{<\alpha}\varphi,\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}^{\varphi^{\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}}_{L}}|\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}^{\varphi^{\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}}_{R}}\right\}.

Since
\varphi\sqsubseteq\psi
, we have
\varphi_{L}^{\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}}\subseteq\psi_{L}^{\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}}
and

\varphi_{R}^{\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}}\subseteq\psi_{R}^{\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}}
, whence

\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}^{\varphi^{\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}}_{L}}<E_{\alpha}\psi<\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}^{\varphi^{\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}}_{R}}.

Furthermore, we have
L_{\alpha}E_{<\alpha}\varphi<\psi
, so
E_{<\alpha}\varphi<E_{\alpha}^{\psi}
. We conclude that
E_{\alpha}^{\varphi}\sqsubseteq E_{\alpha}^{\psi}
.
∎

5.4
Nested truncation

In
[
10
, Section 8]
, the authors prove the well-nestedness axiom

T4
for
\mathbf{No}
by relying on a well-founded partial order

\mbox{}_{\operatorname{BM}}
that is
defined by induction. This relation has the additional property that

\forall a,b\in\mathbf{No}^{\neq},\quad a\mbox{}_{\operatorname{BM}}b\Longrightarrow a\sqsubseteq b.

In this subsection, we define a similar relation
\lesssim
on
\mathbf{No}

that will be instrumental in deriving results on the structure of

(\mathbf{No},(L_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\omega^{\mathbf{On}}})
. However, this
relation does
not
satisfy
a\lesssim b\Longrightarrow a\sqsubseteq b
for all
a,b\in\mathbf{No}
.

Given
a,b\in\mathbf{No}
, we define

\displaystyle a\lesssim b

\displaystyle\xLeftrightarrow{\operatorname{def}}

\displaystyle\exists n\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 11.99998pta\lesssim_{n}b,

where
(\lesssim_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of relations that are
defined by induction on
n
, as follows. For
n=0
, we set
a\lesssim_{0}b
,
if
a\trianglelefteqslant b
or if there exist decompositions

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathfrak{m}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta,

with
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
and
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbf{Mo}
. Assuming
that
\lesssim_{n}
has been defined, we set
a\lesssim_{n+1}b
if we are in
one of the two following configurations:

Configuration I

We may decompose
a
and
b
as

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{u})^{\iota}

(33)

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{v})^{\iota}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta,

(34)

where
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
,
\psi\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ}
,
\alpha\in\omega^{\mathbf{On}}
,
\beta\omega<\alpha
,
\iota\in\{-1,1\}
,

u,v\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}
,

\operatorname{supp}\psi\succ\log E_{\alpha}^{u},\hskip 10.00002ptL_{\beta+1}E_{\alpha}^{v},

and
u\lesssim_{n}v
. If
\alpha=1
, then we also require that
\psi=0
.

Configuration II

We may decompose
a
and
b
as

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathrm{e}^{\psi}

(35)

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathrm{e}^{\psi^{\prime}}\mathfrak{a}^{\iota}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta,

(36)

where
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
,
\psi,\psi^{\prime}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ}
,

\iota\in\{-1,1\}
,
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\omega},\delta\in\mathbf{No}
,
\operatorname{supp}\psi^{\prime}\succ\log\mathfrak{a}
, and
\psi\lesssim_{n}\psi^{\prime}
.

Warning 1

Taking
\alpha=1
in the first configuration, we see that
\lesssim

extends Berarducci and Mantova’s nested truncation ordering. However, the
relation
\lesssim
is neither transitive nor anti-symmetric. Furthermore,
as we already noted above, we do
not
have
\forall a,b\in\mathbf{No},a\lesssim b\Longrightarrow a\sqsubseteq b
.

Lemma 5.26

Let
\alpha\in\omega^{\mathbf{On}}
. Let

a,b\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
be numbers of the form

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}s\mathfrak{n}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta

where
\varphi,\delta\in\mathbf{No}
,
r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
with

\operatorname{sign}(r)=\operatorname{sign}(s)
, and
\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbf{Mo}^{\prec}
. If
\mathfrak{m}^{-1}<E_{\rho}\mathfrak{n}^{-1}

for sufficiently large
\rho<\alpha
, then

b\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}\Longrightarrow a\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}.

Proof.

Let
\nu\in\mathbf{On}
and
\alpha:=\omega^{\nu}
. Assume for
contradiction that
b\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}
and
a\not\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}
. Assume first that
a\vartriangleleft b
, so

b=a\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta
. Then
\operatorname{supp}b\succ\frac{1}{L_{<\alpha}E_{\alpha}b}
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{>}
be such that
a\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}k\mathfrak{d}_{\delta}\geqslant b
. Since
\operatorname{supp}(a\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}k\mathfrak{d}_{\delta})\subseteq\operatorname{supp}b
, we deduce that

\operatorname{supp}(a+k\mathfrak{d}_{\delta})\succ\frac{1}{L_{<\alpha}E_{\alpha}(a+k\mathfrak{d}_{\delta})}
, whence
{a+k\mathfrak{d}_{\delta}}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}
. Modulo replacing

b
by
a+k\mathfrak{d}_{\delta}
, it follow that we may assume without
loss of generality that
\delta=k\mathfrak{p}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}^{>}
and some monomial
\mathfrak{p}
.

On the one hand,
a
is not
\alpha
-truncated, so there are
\mathfrak{q}\in(\operatorname{supp}\varphi)_{\prec}
and
\gamma
with
0<\gamma<\alpha

and
a<L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\gamma}(\mathfrak{q}^{-1})
. We
may choose
\gamma=\omega^{\eta}n
for certain
\eta<\nu
and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{>}
, so
a<L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}n}(\mathfrak{p}^{-1})
. On the other hand,
a+k\mathfrak{p}
is

\alpha
-truncated, so we have

a+k\mathfrak{p}>L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}(n+\mathbb{N}^{>})}(\mathfrak{p}^{-1})>L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}n}(\mathfrak{p}^{-1})>a.

We deduce that
k\mathfrak{p}>L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}(n+\mathbb{N}^{>})}(\mathfrak{p}^{-1})-L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}n}(\mathfrak{p}^{-1})
. If
\nu
is a successor, then
choosing
{\eta=\nu_{-}}
, we obtain
k\mathfrak{p}>L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}n}(\mathfrak{p}^{-1})+\mathbb{N}^{>}-L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}n}(\mathfrak{p}^{-1})
, so
k\mathfrak{p}\succ 1
: a contradiction.
Otherwise,
k\mathfrak{p}>\ell_{[\omega^{\eta+1},\alpha)}^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{p}^{-1}
by
[
7
, (2.4)]
, where
\ell_{[\omega^{\eta+1},\alpha)}:=\prod_{\omega^{\eta+1}\leqslant\gamma<\alpha}\ell_{\gamma}
. Thus
k^{-1}\mathfrak{p}^{-1}<{\ell_{[\omega^{\eta+1},\alpha)}\circ\mathfrak{p}^{-1}}
, whence
k^{-1}\ell_{0}<\ell_{[\omega^{\eta+1},\alpha)}
: a contradiction.

We now treat the general case. By a similar argument as above, we may assume
without loss of generality that
b=\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}s\mathfrak{n}
. Assume
that
b\leqslant a
. Since
a
is not
\alpha
-truncated, there exists a

\gamma<\alpha
with
\mathfrak{m}\prec(L_{\gamma}E_{\alpha}a)^{-1}\leqslant(L_{\gamma}E_{\alpha}b)^{-1}
, whence
\mathfrak{m}^{-1}\succ L_{\gamma}E_{\alpha}b
. But
b
is
\alpha
-
truncated, so

\mathfrak{n}^{-1}\prec L_{<\alpha}E_{\alpha}b
. In particular

\mathfrak{n}^{-1}\prec L_{\gamma}E_{\alpha}b
, so our hypothesis

\mathfrak{m}^{-1}<L_{\rho}\mathfrak{n}^{-1}
implies
that
\mathfrak{m}^{-1}\prec L_{\rho}L_{\gamma}E_{\alpha}b\preccurlyeq L_{\gamma}E_{\alpha}b
: a contradiction.

Assume now that
b>a
. As in the first part of the proof, there are
\eta<\nu
and
n<n^{\prime}<\omega
with
\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}s\mathfrak{n}>L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}n^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{n}^{-1})
and

L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}n}(\mathfrak{m}^{-1})>\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathfrak{m}
. Recall that
\mathfrak{m}^{-1}<E_{\rho}\mathfrak{n}^{-1}
for sufficiently large
\rho<\alpha
. Take
\eta<\nu
and
n^{\prime}<\omega
such that

\displaystyle L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}n^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{n}^{-1})

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}(n+1)}(\mathfrak{m}^{-1})

\displaystyle L_{\omega^{\eta}}\mathfrak{m}^{-1}

\displaystyle\prec

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{-1}\quad\text{if $\nu$ is a limit.}

(37)

Then
b-a>L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}(n+1)}(\mathfrak{m}^{-1})-L_{\alpha}^{\mathord{\uparrow}\omega^{\eta}n}(\mathfrak{m}^{-1})
. If
\nu
is a successor, then choosing
\eta=\nu_{-}

yields
b-a>1
, which contradicts the fact that
\mathfrak{m}
and

\mathfrak{n}
are infinitesimal. So
\nu
is a limit. Writing
\mathfrak{q}:=\max(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n})
, we have
b-a\asymp\mathfrak{q}
. As in the first part of the proof, we obtain
\mathfrak{q}\succcurlyeq{\ell_{[\omega^{\eta+1},\alpha)}^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{m}^{-1}}
, so
\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\preccurlyeq\ell_{[\omega^{\eta+1},\alpha)}\circ\mathfrak{m}^{-1}\prec\mathfrak{m}^{-1}
. In view of
(
37
), we also obtain
\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\prec\mathfrak{n}^{-1}
, so
\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\prec\max(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n})^{-1}
: a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 5.27

Let
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\omega^{\mathbf{On}}

with
\alpha^{\prime}\geqslant\alpha
. For
u,v\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
, we
have

\displaystyle L_{\alpha}u<\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}v

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle L_{\alpha^{\prime}}E_{\alpha}u<\mathcal{E}_{\alpha^{\prime}}E_{\alpha}v.

Proof.

Assume that
L_{\alpha}u<\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}v
. Let
h\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
and let
h^{\operatorname{inv}}
be its functional inverse in

\mathcal{E}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
. We have
h^{\operatorname{inv}}<E_{\alpha^{\prime}}H_{2}L_{\alpha^{\prime}}
by (
24
,
25
), whence
h>E_{\alpha^{\prime}}H_{1/2}L_{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Furthermore,
u<E_{\alpha}\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}v
, so

E_{\alpha}u<E_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}v.

(38)

We want to prove that
E_{\alpha}u<(E_{\alpha^{\prime}}hE_{\alpha})v
. By
(
38
), it is enough to prove that there is a
g\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}
such that the inequality
E_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}g\leqslant E_{\alpha^{\prime}}hE_{\alpha}
holds on
\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
.

Assume that
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
. Setting
g:=H_{1/2}\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}
, we have
L_{\alpha}hE_{\alpha}>g
, whence

E_{\alpha}g\leqslant hE_{\alpha}
, and
E_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}g\leqslant E_{\alpha}hE_{\alpha}
.

Assume that
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha
. We have
E_{\alpha^{\prime}}H_{1/2}>H_{2}
so

E_{\alpha^{\prime}2}H_{1/2}>E_{\alpha^{\prime}}H_{2}>E_{\alpha}E_{\alpha^{\prime}}

by (
22
). Thus
{E_{\alpha^{\prime}}h>E_{\alpha^{\prime}2}H_{1/2}L_{\alpha^{\prime}}>E_{\alpha}}
. Consequently,
E_{\alpha^{\prime}}hE_{\alpha}>E_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}
, as claimed.
∎

Given
a,b\in\mathbf{No}
, we write
[a\leftrightarrow b]
for the interval

[\min(a,b),\max(a,b)]
.

Proposition 5.28

For
a,b,c\in\mathbf{No}
with
a\lesssim c
and
b\in[a\leftrightarrow c]
, any infinite path in
a
shares
a subpath with
b
.

Proof.

We prove this by induction on
n
with
a\lesssim_{n}c
. Let
P
be an
infinite path in
a
. Assume that
a\lesssim_{0}c
. If
a\trianglelefteqslant c
, then we have
a\trianglelefteqslant b
so
P
is a
path in
b
. Otherwise, there are
\varphi,\delta\in\mathbf{No}
,
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
and
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbf{Mo}
with
a=\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathfrak{m}
and
c=\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathfrak{m}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta
. Then
b=\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}s\mathfrak{n}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}t
for certain

t\in\mathbf{No}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
and
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbf{Mo}
with
s\mathfrak{n}\in[\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathfrak{m}\leftrightarrow r\mathfrak{m}]
. We must have
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{m}
.
If
P
is a path in
\varphi
, then it is a path in
b
. Otherwise, it is a
path in
\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathfrak{m}
, so
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath in

s\mathfrak{m}
, hence in
b
.

We now assume that
a\lesssim_{n}c
where
n>0
and that the result holds
for all
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in\mathbf{No}
and
k<n
with
a^{\prime}\lesssim_{k}c^{\prime}
and

b^{\prime}\in[a^{\prime}\leftrightarrow c^{\prime}]
. Assume first that
(a,c)
is in

Configuration I
, and write

\begin{array}[]{rcl}a&=&\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{u})^{\iota}\\
c&=&\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{v})^{\iota}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta\end{array}\mathpunct{\text{\quad with\quad}}u\lesssim_{n-1}v.

Then we can write
b=\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}s\mathfrak{m}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}t
like in the
case when
n=0
. If
P
is a path in
\varphi
, then it is a path in
b
.
So we may assume that
P
is a path in
\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{u})^{\iota}
. Note that we have
\mathfrak{m}\in[\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{u})^{\iota}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{v})^{\iota}]
. Setting
\mathfrak{n}:=(\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{e}^{-\psi})^{\iota}\in[E_{\alpha}^{u}\leftrightarrow L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{v}]
, we observe that
\operatorname{supp}\log\mathfrak{n}\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi
, whence
\mathrm{e}^{\psi}\mathfrak{n}^{\iota}
is the
hyperserial expansion of
\mathfrak{m}
. If
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a path in

\psi
, then it is a path in
\mathfrak{m}
.

Suppose that
P_{\nearrow 1}
is not a path in
\psi
. Assume first that

\alpha=1
, so
\psi=0
,
\beta=0
, and
P
is a path in

(E_{1}^{u})^{\iota}
. Then Lemma
5.14
implies that

P_{\nearrow 1}
is a subpath in
\iota u
, so
P_{\nearrow 2}
is a subpath
in
u
. Otherwise, consider the hyperserial expansion
E_{\alpha}^{u}=L_{\beta^{\prime}}E_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{w}
,
E_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{w}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\setminus L_{<\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha^{\prime}\omega}
of
E_{\alpha}^{u}
.
Since
P_{\nearrow 1}
is not a path in
\psi
, it must be a path in
w
.
The number
L_{\beta^{\prime}}E_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{w}
is
L_{<\alpha}
-
atomic, so we
must have
\alpha^{\prime}\geqslant\alpha
and
\beta^{\prime}\geq\!\!\!\geq\alpha_{/\omega}
.
There are
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\beta^{\prime\prime}\gg\alpha_{/\omega}
such that

\beta^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime\prime}+\alpha_{/\omega}n
. Therefore
u=L_{\beta^{\prime\prime}+\alpha}E_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{w}-n
. It follows by
Corollary

5.17
that

P_{\nearrow 1}
shares a subpath with
u
, whence so does
P
.

Let
z:=\sharp_{\alpha}(L_{\alpha}\mathfrak{n})
. Recall that

\mathfrak{n}\in[E_{\alpha}^{u}\leftrightarrow L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{v}]
, so

L_{\alpha}\mathfrak{n}\in[u\leftrightarrow L_{\alpha}L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{v}]
. Now (
22
) implies that
L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{v}\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}[E_{\alpha}^{v}]
, so
L_{\alpha}L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{v}\in L_{\alpha}\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}[E_{\alpha}^{v}]=\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}[v]
. The function
\sharp_{\alpha}=\pi_{\mathbf{Smp}_{\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}}}
is non-decreasing, so

z=\sharp_{\alpha}(L_{\alpha}\mathfrak{n})\in[u\leftrightarrow\sharp_{\alpha}(L_{\alpha}L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{v})]=[u\leftrightarrow v].

But
u\lesssim_{n-1}v
, so the induction hypothesis yields that

P_{\nearrow 2}
, and thus
P
, shares a subpath with
z
. We deduce with
Lemma
5.15
that
P
shares a subpath
with
\mathfrak{n}
, hence with
b
.

Assume now that
(a,c)
is in
Configuration II
, and write

\begin{array}[]{rcl}a&=&\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathrm{e}^{\psi}\\
c&=&\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathrm{e}^{\psi^{\prime}}\mathfrak{a}^{\iota}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta\end{array}\mathpunct{\text{\quad with\quad}}\psi\lesssim_{n-1}\psi^{\prime}.

Note that we also have
\psi\lesssim_{n-1}\psi^{\prime}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\iota\log\mathfrak{a}
. We may again assume that
P_{\nearrow 1}
is a path in

\psi
. Write
b=\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}s^{\prime}\mathfrak{q}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}t^{\prime}
, where
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
,
t^{\prime}\in\mathbf{No}
, and
\mathfrak{q}\in[\mathrm{e}^{\psi}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{e}^{\psi^{\prime}}\mathfrak{a}^{\iota}]\cap\mathbf{Mo}
. Then
\log\mathfrak{q}\in[\psi\leftrightarrow\psi^{\prime}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\iota\log\mathfrak{a}]
where
\psi\lesssim_{n-1}\psi^{\prime}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\iota\log\mathfrak{a}
. We deduce by induction that
P
shares a subpath
with
\log\mathfrak{q}
. By Lemma
5.15
, it follows
that
P
shares a subpath with
\mathfrak{q}
, hence with
b
. This
concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.29

Let
\lambda,\alpha\in\omega^{\mathbf{On}}

and
\beta\in\mathbf{On}
with
\beta\omega<\alpha
. Let
a\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\lambda}
be of the form

a=\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{b})^{\iota}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta,

with
\varphi\in\mathbf{No}
,
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
,
\psi\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ}
,
b\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}
,
\iota\in\{-1,1\}
,
\delta\in\mathbf{No}
and
\log L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{b}\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi
. Consider an infinite path
P
in
c\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha}
with
c\lesssim b
.

i.

If
\log E_{\alpha}^{c}\nprec\operatorname{supp}\psi
, then
P
shares a subpath with
\psi
.

ii.

If
\log E_{\alpha}^{c}\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi
and
\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{c})^{\iota}\nprec\operatorname{supp}\varphi
, then
P
shares a subpath with
\varphi
.

iii.

If
\log E_{\alpha}^{c}\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi
and
\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{c})^{\iota}\prec\operatorname{supp}\varphi
and
a^{\prime}:=\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{c})^{\iota}\not\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\lambda}
,
then
P
shares a subpath with
\varphi
.

Proof.

i
. If
\log E_{\alpha}^{c}\nprec\operatorname{supp}\psi
, then we have
\psi\neq 0
, so
\alpha>1
. Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\operatorname{supp}\psi
with
\log E_{\alpha}^{c}\succcurlyeq\mathfrak{m}
. Since

\log E_{\alpha}^{c}
and
\mathfrak{m}
are monomials, we have
\mathfrak{m}\leqslant\log E_{\alpha}^{c}
, whence
\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{m}}\leqslant E_{\alpha}^{c}
. Our assumption that
\mathfrak{m}\in\operatorname{supp}\psi\succ\log L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{b}
also implies
\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{m}}\leqslant L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{b}
. Hence
\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{m}}\in[E_{\alpha}^{c}\leftrightarrow L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{b}]
. Now
P
shares a subpath with

E_{\alpha}^{c}
, by Lemma
5.15
. Since
E_{\alpha}^{c}\lesssim L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{b}
, Proposition
5.28

next implies that
P
shares a subpath with
\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{m}}
. Using
Lemma
5.14
, we conclude that
P
shares a subpath with

\mathfrak{m}
, and hence with
\psi
.

ii
. Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\operatorname{supp}\varphi

with
\mathfrak{m}\preccurlyeq\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{c})^{\iota}
. It
is enough to prove that
P
shares a subpath with
\mathfrak{m}
. Since

\mathfrak{m}
,
\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{b})^{\iota}
, and

\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{c})^{\iota}
are monomials, we have

\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{b})^{\iota}\leqslant\mathfrak{m}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{c})^{\iota}
. Let
\mathfrak{n}:=(\mathrm{e}^{-\psi}\mathfrak{m})^{\iota}
, so that
\mathfrak{n}\in[L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{b}\leftrightarrow E_{\alpha}^{c}]
. In particular, we
have
\operatorname{supp}\psi\succ\log\mathfrak{n}\succ 1
. Moreover

E_{\alpha}^{c}\lesssim L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{b}
, so using
Lemma
5.15
and
Proposition
5.28
, we deduce in the same way as above
that
P
shares a subpath with
\mathfrak{n}
. If
\mathfrak{n}\not\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\omega}
, then
\mathfrak{m}=\mathrm{e}^{\psi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\iota\log\mathfrak{n}}
is the hyperserial expansion of
\mathfrak{m}
, so
P
shares
a subpath with
\mathfrak{m}
. If
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\omega}
,
then the hyperserial expansion of
\mathfrak{n}
must be of the form

\mathfrak{n}=E_{\beta^{\prime}}E_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{u}
, since otherwise
\log\mathfrak{n}
would have at least two elements in its support. We deduce
that
P
shares a subpath with
u
and that the hyperserial expansion of

\mathfrak{m}
is
\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\beta^{\prime}}E_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{u})^{\iota}
.
Therefore
P
shares a subpath with
\mathfrak{m}
.

iii
. We assume that
a^{\prime}
is not

\lambda
-truncated whereas
\log E_{\alpha}^{c}\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi
and

\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{c})^{\iota}\prec\operatorname{supp}\varphi
. If

\lambda=1
, then we must have
\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{c})^{\iota}\preccurlyeq 1
, which means that
\psi<0
or that
\psi=0
and
\iota=-1
. But then
\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{b})^{\iota}\preccurlyeq 1
: a contradiction.

Assume that
\lambda>1
. By Lemma
5.26
, we may
assume without loss of generality that
\delta=0
. The assumption on
a^{\prime}

and the fact that
a\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
imply that
\varphi
is
non-zero. Write

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(E_{\alpha}^{c})^{\iota}\text{\quad and}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{b})^{\iota}.

So
a=\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathfrak{q}
and
a^{\prime}=\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r)\mathfrak{p}
. Note that
\mathfrak{p}
must be infinitesimal since
a^{\prime}

is not
\lambda
-
truncated. Thus
\mathfrak{q}
is also
infinitesimal. By Lemma
5.26
, we deduce that
E_{<\lambda}\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\prec\mathfrak{p}^{-1}
. We have

\sharp_{\lambda}(a^{\prime})\vartriangleleft a^{\prime}
, so
\sharp_{\lambda}(a^{\prime})=\varphi
, since
a
and
\varphi\vartriangleleft a
are both

\lambda
-truncated. Since
a^{\prime}
is not
\lambda
-
truncated, there is
an ordinal
\gamma<\lambda
with
\mathfrak{p}\prec(L_{\gamma}E_{\lambda}^{\varphi})^{-1}
. If
\varphi\geqslant a
, then
\mathfrak{q}\succ(L_{<\lambda}E_{\lambda}^{a})^{-1}
, because
a
is

\lambda
-truncated. Thus
\mathfrak{q}\succ(L_{<\lambda}E_{\lambda}^{\varphi})^{-1}
. If
\varphi<a
, then
\varphi+(L_{<\lambda}E_{\lambda}^{\varphi})^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}[\varphi]<\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}[a]\ni a=\varphi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r\mathfrak{q}
, because

\varphi
and
a
are
\lambda
-truncated. Now
r>0
, since
\varphi<a
.
We again deduce that
\mathfrak{q}\succ(L_{<\lambda}E_{\lambda}^{\varphi})^{-1}
.

In both cases, we have
L_{\gamma}E_{\lambda}^{\varphi}\in[\mathfrak{p}^{-1}\leftrightarrow\mathfrak{q}^{-1}]
where

\mathfrak{p}^{-1}\lesssim\mathfrak{q}^{-1}
, so
P
shares a subpath
with
L_{\gamma}E_{\lambda}^{\varphi}
, by
Proposition
5.28
. It follows by

Corollary

5.17
that
P
shares a subpath with
\varphi
.
∎

5.5
Well-nestedness

We now prove that every number is well-nested. Throughout this subsection,
P

will be an infinite path inside a number
a\in\mathbf{No}
. At the beginning
of Section
5.2
we have shown how to attach sequences

(r_{P,i})_{i<\omega}
,
(\mathfrak{m}_{P,i})_{i<\omega}
,
etc. to this path. In order to alleviate notations, we will
abbreviate
r_{i}:=r_{P,i}
,
\mathfrak{m}_{i}:=\mathfrak{m}_{P,i}
,
u_{i}:=u_{P,i}
,
\psi_{i}:=\psi_{P,i}
,
\iota_{i}:=\iota_{P,i}
,
\alpha_{i}:=\alpha_{P,i}
, and
\beta_{i}:=\beta_{P,i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

We start with a technical lemma that will be used to show that the existence
of a bad path
P
in
a
implies the existence of a bad path in a strictly
simpler number than
a
.

Lemma 5.30

Let
a\in\mathbf{No}
, let
P
be an infinite
path in
a
and let
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that every index
k\leqslant i

is good for
(P,a)
. For
k\leqslant i
, let
\varphi_{k}:=(u_{k})_{\succ\mathfrak{m}_{k}}
,
\varepsilon_{k}:=r_{k}
, and
\rho_{k}:=(u_{k})_{\prec\mathfrak{m}_{k}}
, so that
\varepsilon_{0},\ldots,\varepsilon_{i-1}\in\{-1,1\}
and

\displaystyle u_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{u_{k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}\hskip 0.0pt(k<i)

\displaystyle u_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i+1}}(L_{\beta_{i}}E_{\alpha_{i}}^{u_{i+1}})^{\iota_{i}}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\rho_{i}.

Let
\chi\in\{0,1\}
and let
c_{i}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{i-1}}
be a number with
c_{i}\lesssim u_{i}
and

c_{i}=\varphi_{i}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\chi\operatorname{sign}(r_{i})\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i+1}}\mathfrak{p}^{\iota_{i}},

(39)

for a certain
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbf{Mo}^{\succcurlyeq}
with
\log\mathfrak{p}\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i+1}
,
\mathfrak{p}\sqsubseteq E_{\alpha_{i}}^{u_{i+1}}
and
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[E_{\alpha_{i}}^{u_{i+1}}]
whenever
\psi_{i+1}=0
. For
k=i-1,\ldots,0
, we define

c_{k}:=\varphi_{k}+\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{c_{k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}

(40)

Assume that
P
shares a subpath with
c_{i}
. If
P
shares no subpath with
any of the numbers
\varphi_{0},\psi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{i-1},\psi_{i}
,
then we have
c_{0}\sqsubseteq a
, and
P
shares a subpath with
c_{0}
.

Proof.

Using backward induction on

, let us prove for

that

\displaystyle L_{\alpha_{k}}c_{k+1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{k}}u_{k+1}

(41)

\displaystyle\log E_{\alpha_{k}}^{c_{k+1}}

\displaystyle\prec

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\psi_{k+1}

(42)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{c_{k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}

\displaystyle\prec

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{k}

(43)

\displaystyle c_{k}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle u_{k}

(44)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\mathord{\text{$P$ shares a subpath}}

with

\displaystyle c_{k+1}

(45)

\displaystyle c_{k+1}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{k}}

(46)

\displaystyle c_{k+1}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq

\displaystyle u_{k+1}

(47)

and that (

. Given

We first treat the case when

or

From our assumption that

.

)

)

, since

and

by

and

, so (

It suffices to prove that

, since

\displaystyle E_{\alpha_{k}}^{c_{k+1}}\sqsubseteq E_{\alpha_{k}}^{u_{k+1}}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{c_{k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}\sqsubseteq(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{u_{k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}

(Lemma

)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{c_{k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{u_{k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}

(Lemma

)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{c_{k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}\sqsubseteq\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{u_{k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{c_{k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}\sqsubseteq\varphi_{k}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{u_{k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}

(Lemma
5.20
)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle c_{k}\sqsubseteq u_{k}.

Assume that
\alpha_{k}>1
and recall that

\displaystyle c_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{c_{k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}

\displaystyle c_{k+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{k+1}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{k+1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+2}}(E_{\alpha_{k+1}}^{c_{k+2}})^{\iota_{k+1}}.

By Lemma
5.25
, it suffices to prove that

and that

)
{}_{\textrm{$k$}}
. By (

)
{}_{\textrm{$k$}}
, we have

L_{\alpha_{k}}c_{k+1}<\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{k}}u_{k+1}
. Therefore

c_{k+1}<E_{\alpha_{k}}\frac{1}{2}u_{k+1}<L_{<\alpha_{k}}E_{\alpha_{k}}u_{k+1}
by Lemma
5.25
. This yields the
result.

Assume now that
\alpha_{k}=1
. For
d=0,\ldots,i
, let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{d}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{c_{d}-\varphi_{d}}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}_{d}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{u_{d}-\varphi_{d}}.

We will prove, by a second descending induction on
d=i,\ldots,k-1
,
that the monomials
\mathfrak{c}_{d}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{d}
satisfy the
premises of Lemma
5.24
, i.e.

\mathfrak{c}_{d},\mathfrak{u}_{d}\succ 1
,
\mathfrak{c}_{d}\in\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[\mathfrak{u}_{d}]
, and
\mathfrak{c}_{d}\sqsubseteq\mathfrak{u}_{d}
. It will then follow by Lemma
5.24
that

\mathrm{e}^{c_{k}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{e}^{u_{k}}
, thus concluding the proof.

If
d=i
, then
\operatorname{supp}c_{i},\operatorname{supp}u_{i}\succ 1
, because

\alpha_{i-1}=1
. In particular
\mathfrak{c}_{i},\mathfrak{u}_{i}\succ 1
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{c}_{i}\sqsubseteq\mathfrak{u}_{i}
follows from
our assumption that
\mathfrak{p}\sqsubseteq E_{\alpha_{i}}^{u_{i+1}}
,
the fact that
E_{\alpha_{i}}^{u_{i+1}}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{i}}[E_{\alpha_{i}}^{u_{i+1}}]\ni L_{\beta_{i}}E_{\alpha_{i}}^{u_{i+1}}
, and
Lemmas
5.22
and
5.21
. If
\psi_{i+1}\neq 0
,
then we have
\mathfrak{c}_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[\mathfrak{u}_{i}]

because
\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i+1}\succ\log\mathfrak{p},\log E_{\alpha_{i}}^{u_{i+1}}
. Otherwise, we have
\mathfrak{c}_{i}=\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[E_{\alpha_{i}}^{u_{i+1}}]=\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[\mathfrak{u}_{i}]
.

Now assume that
d<i
, that the result holds for
d+1
, and that

\alpha_{d}=1
. Again
\alpha_{d}=1
implies that
\mathfrak{c}_{d+1},\mathfrak{u}_{d+1}\succ 1
. The relation
c_{d+1}\sqsubseteq u_{d+1}
and Lemmas
5.18
,
5.19
,
and
5.20
imply that
\mathfrak{c}_{d+1}\sqsubseteq\mathfrak{u}_{d+1}
. If
\psi_{d+2}\neq 0
, then
\mathfrak{c}_{d+1}\in\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[\mathfrak{u}_{d+1}]

by (
42
)
{}_{\textrm{$d+1$}}
. Otherwise, we have
\iota_{d+1}=1
, because
c_{d}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,1}
. Since
\alpha_{d}=1
, the
number
u_{d+1}=\varphi_{d+1}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{d+1}E_{\alpha_{d+1}}^{u_{d+2}}
is not tail-atomic, so we must
have
\alpha_{d+1}=1
. This entails that
\mathfrak{c}_{d+1}=\mathrm{e}^{c_{d+2}}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{d+1}=\mathrm{e}^{u_{d+2}}
. By
the induction hypothesis at
d+1
, we have
\mathfrak{c}_{d+2}\in\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[\mathfrak{u}_{d+2}]
. We deduce that
c_{d+2}\in\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[u_{d+2}]
, so

\mathfrak{c}_{d+1}\in\exp\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[u_{d+2}]=\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[\mathrm{e}^{u_{d+2}}]=\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[\mathfrak{u}_{d+1}].

It follows by induction that (
47
)
{}_{\textrm{$k-1$}}
is valid.

This concludes our inductive proof. The lemma follows from
(
47
)
{}_{\textrm{$-1$}}
and (
45
)
{}_{\textrm{$-1$}}
.
∎

We are now in a position to prove our first main theorem.

Proof of
Theorem

1.1

Assume for contradiction that the theorem is false. Let
a
be a

\sqsubseteq
-minimal ill-nested number and let
P
be a bad path in
a
.
Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
be the smallest bad index in
(P,a)
. As in
Lemma
5.30
, we define
\varphi_{k}:=(u_{k})_{\prec\mathfrak{m}_{k}}
,
\rho_{k}:=(u_{k})_{\succ\mathfrak{m}_{k}}
, and

\varepsilon_{k}:=r_{k}
for all
k\leqslant i
. We may assume that
i>0
, otherwise the number
c_{0}:=\varphi_{0}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r_{0})\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{1}}(E_{\alpha_{0}}^{u_{1}})^{\iota_{0}}
is ill-nested and
satisfies
c_{0}\sqsubset a
: a contradiction.

Assume for contradiction that there is a
j<i
such that
\varphi_{j}
or

\psi_{j+1}
is ill-nested. Set
\chi:=0
if
\varphi_{j}
is
ill-nested and
\chi:=1
otherwise. If
\chi=1
, then
P
cannot
share a subpath with
\varphi_{j}
, so
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j}\succ\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{j+1}}
by Lemma
5.29
, and
\varphi_{j}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{j}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{j+1}}
is ill-nested. In general, it
follows that
c_{j}:=\varphi_{j}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\chi\varepsilon_{j}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{j+1}}
is ill-nested. Let
Q
be a bad path in
c_{j}
and set

P^{\prime}:=(P(0),\ldots,P(j-1))\ast Q
. Then we may apply
Lemma
5.30
to
j
,
c_{j}
, and
P^{\prime}
in the roles of
i
,

c_{i}
, and
P
. Since
c_{j}\neq u_{j}
, this yields an ill-nested number
c_{0}\sqsubset a
: a contradiction.

Therefore the numbers
\varphi_{0},\psi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{i-1},\psi_{i}

are well-nested. Since
i
is bad for
(P,a)
, one of the four cases listed
in Definition
5.10
must occur. We set

d_{i}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\varphi_{i}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r_{i})\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i+1}}&&\text{if Definition\penalty\ \ref{def-good-path}(\ref{def-good-path-4})
occurs}\\
\varphi_{i}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\operatorname{sign}(r_{i})\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i+1}}(E_{\alpha_{i}}^{u_{i+1}})^{\iota_{i}}&&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

By construction, we have
d_{i}\lesssim u_{i}
. Furthermore
P
shares a
subpath with
d_{i}
, so there exists a bad path
Q
in
d_{i}
. We have
d_{i}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{j-1}}
by
Lemma
5.26
. If
Definition
5.10
(
4
) occurs, then we must
have
\psi_{i+1}\neq 0
so
d_{i}
is written as in (
39
) with

d_{i}
in the role of
c_{i}
and
\mathfrak{p}=\chi=1
. Otherwise,
d_{i}
is
as in (
39
) for
\mathfrak{p}=E_{\alpha_{i}}^{u_{i+1}}
. Setting

P^{\prime}:=(P(0),\ldots,P(i-1))\ast Q
, it follows that we may apply
Lemma
5.30
to
d_{i}
and
P^{\prime}
in the roles of
c_{i}
and

P
. We conclude that there exists an ill-nested number
d_{0}\sqsubset a
: a
contradiction.

\Box

6
Surreal substructures of nested numbers

In the previous section, we have examined the nature of infinite paths in
surreal numbers and shown that they are ultimately “well-behaved”. In this
section, we work in the opposite direction and show how to construct surreal
numbers that contain infinite paths of a specified kind. We follow the same
method as in
[
5
, Section 8]
.

Let us briefly outline the main ideas. Our aim is to construct “nested
numbers” that correspond to nested expressions like

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\omega}+\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\log\omega}-\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\log\log\omega}+\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\log\log\log\omega}-\mathrm{e}^{{\mathinner{\mskip 1.0mu\raise 1.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{.}}\mskip 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mskip 2.0mu\raise 7.0pt\hbox{.}\mskip 1.0mu}}}}}}

(48)

Nested expressions of this kind will be presented through so-called

coding sequences

\Sigma
. Once we have fixed such a coding sequence

\Sigma
, numbers
a
of the form (
48
) need to satisfy a sequence
of natural inequalities: for any
r\in\mathbb{R}
with
r>1
, we require
that

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\frac{1}{r}\sqrt{\omega}&<&a&<&r\sqrt{\omega}\\
\sqrt{\omega}+\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{r}\sqrt{\log\omega}}&<&a&<&\sqrt{\omega}+\mathrm{e}^{r\sqrt{\log\omega}}\\
\sqrt{\omega}+\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\log\omega}-\mathrm{e}^{r\sqrt{\log\log\omega}}}&<&a&<&\sqrt{\omega}+\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\log\omega}-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{r}\sqrt{\log\log\omega}}}\\
&&\vdots&&\end{array}

Numbers that satisfy these constraints are said to be
admissible
.
Under suitable conditions, the class
Ad
of admissible numbers
forms a convex surreal substructure. This will be detailed in
Sections
6.1

and
6.2
, where we will also introduce
suitable coordinates

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}a_{;0}&=&\sqrt{\omega}+\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\log\omega}-\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\log\log\omega}+\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\log\log\log\omega}-\mathrm{e}^{{\mathinner{\mskip 1.0mu\raise 1.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{.}}\mskip 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mskip 2.0mu\raise 7.0pt\hbox{.}\mskip 1.0mu}}}}}}&=&a\\
a_{;1}&=&\sqrt{\log\omega}-\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\log\log\omega}+\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\log\log\log\omega}-\mathrm{e}^{{\mathinner{\mskip 1.0mu\raise 1.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{.}}\mskip 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mskip 2.0mu\raise 7.0pt\hbox{.}\mskip 1.0mu}}}}}&=&\log\left(a_{;0}-\sqrt{\omega}\right)\\
a_{;2}&=&\sqrt{\log\log\omega}+\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\log\log\log\omega}-\mathrm{e}^{{\mathinner{\mskip 1.0mu\raise 1.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{.}}\mskip 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mskip 2.0mu\raise 7.0pt\hbox{.}\mskip 1.0mu}}}}&=&\log\left(\sqrt{\log\omega}-a_{;1}\right)\\
&&\vdots&&\end{array}

for working with numbers in
\mathbf{Ad}
.

The notation (
48
) also suggests that each of the numbers
a_{;0}-\sqrt{\omega}
,
\sqrt{\log\omega}-a_{;1}
,
\ldots
should be a
monomial. An admissible number
a\in\mathbf{Ad}
is said to be

nested
if this is indeed the case. The main result of this section is

Theorem

1.2
, i.e. that the class
\mathbf{Ne}
of
nested numbers forms a surreal substructure. In other words, the
notation (
48
) is ambiguous, but can be disambiguated using a
single surreal parameter.

6.1
Coding sequences

Definition 6.1

Let
\Sigma:=(\varphi_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\psi_{i},\iota_{i},\alpha_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in(\mathbf{No}\times\{-1,1\}\times\mathbf{No}\times\{-1,1\}\times\omega^{\mathbf{On}})^{\mathbb{N}}
. We say that
\Sigma
is a

coding sequence
if for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

a)

\psi_{i}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ}
;

b)

\varphi_{i+1}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{i}}\cup\{0\}
;

c)

(\alpha_{i}=1)\Longrightarrow(\psi_{i}=0\wedge(\psi_{i+1}=0\Longrightarrow\alpha_{i+1}=1))
;

d)

(\varphi_{i+1}=\psi_{i+1}=0)\Longrightarrow(\alpha_{i}\geqslant\alpha_{i+1}\wedge\varepsilon_{i+1}=\iota_{i+1}=1)
;

e)

\exists j>i,(\varphi_{j}\neq 0\vee\psi_{j}\neq 0)
.

Taking
\alpha_{i}=1
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we obtain a
reformulation of the notion of coding sequences
in
[
5
, Section 8.1]
. If
\Sigma=(\varphi_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\psi_{i},\iota_{i},\alpha_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a coding sequence and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then we write

\Sigma_{\nearrow k}:=(\varphi_{k+i},\varepsilon_{k+i},\psi_{k+i},\iota_{k+i},\alpha_{k+i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}},

which is also a coding sequence.

Lemma 6.2

Let
P
be an infinite path in a number
a\in\mathbf{No}
without any bad index for
a
. Let
\varphi_{0}:=a_{\succ\mathfrak{m}_{P,0}}
and
\varphi_{i}:=(a_{P,i})_{\succ\mathfrak{m}_{P,i}}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}^{>}
.
Then
\Sigma_{P}:=(\varphi_{i},r_{P,i},\psi_{P,i+1},\iota_{P,i},\alpha_{P,i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a coding sequence.

Proof.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We have
r_{P,i}\in\{-1,1\}
because
i
is a
good index for
(P,a)
. We have
\psi_{P,i+1}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ}

and
a_{P,i+1}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{i}}
by the definition of
hyperserial expansions. If
i>0
and
\varphi_{i}\neq 0
, then we have

\varphi_{i}\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
because
a_{P,i}\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
by the definition of paths. Lemma
5.26

also yields
\varphi_{i}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{i}}
. This proves the
conditions
a
) and
b
) for coding sequences. Assume that
\alpha_{i}=1
.
Then by the definition of hyperserial expansions, we have
\psi_{P,i+1}=0
and
u_{P,i+1}=a_{P,i+1}
is not tail-atomic. Assume that

\psi_{P,i+2}=0
. Then
\operatorname{supp}u_{P,i+1}\succ 1
so

\iota_{P,i+2}=1
. We have
u_{P,i+1}=\varphi_{i+1}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}r_{P,i+1}\mathfrak{a}
where
\mathfrak{a}:=E_{\alpha_{P,i+1}}^{u_{P,i+2}}
and
u_{P,i+1}
is not tail-atomic. This implies that

\mathfrak{a}
is not log-atomic, so
\alpha_{P,i+1}=1
. Thus
c
) is
valid.

Assume that
\varphi_{i+1}=\psi_{P,i+2}=0
. Recall that
a_{P,i+1}=r_{P,i+1}(E_{\alpha_{P,i+1}}^{u_{P,i+2}})^{\iota_{P,i+1}}=u_{P,i+1}\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
, so
r_{P,i+1}=\iota_{P,i+1}=1
. Since
E_{\alpha_{P,i}}^{u_{P,i+1}}\not\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{P,i}\omega}
, we have
u_{P,i+1}\not\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{P,i}\omega}
, whence
\alpha_{P,i+1}\leqslant\alpha_{P,i}
. This proves
d
).

Assume now for contradiction that there is an
i_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
with

\varphi_{P,j}=\psi_{P,j+1}=0
for all
j>i_{0}
. By
d
), we have

r_{P,j}=\iota_{P,j}=1
for all
j>i_{0}
, and the sequence

(\alpha_{P,j})_{j>i_{0}}
is non-increasing, hence eventually constant.
Let
i_{1}>i_{0}
with
\alpha_{P,i_{1}}=\alpha_{P,j}
for all
j>i_{1}
.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
a_{P,i_{1}}=E_{\alpha_{P,i_{1}}k}a_{P,i_{1}+k}
so
a_{P,i_{1}}\in\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}E_{\alpha_{P,i_{1}}k}\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{P,i_{1}}}=\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{P,i_{1}}\omega}
.
Therefore
E_{\alpha_{P,i_{1}}}^{a_{P,i_{1}+1}}
is
L_{<\alpha_{P,i_{1}+1}\omega}
-
atomic: a contradiction. We deduce that
e
) holds as
well.
∎

We next fix some notations. For all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
with
i\leqslant j
, we define partial functions
\Phi_{i}
,
\Phi_{i;}
and
\Phi_{j;i}
on

\mathbf{No}
by

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Phi_{i}(a)&:=&\varphi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i-1}}(E_{\alpha_{i-1}}a)^{\iota_{i-1}}\text{},\\
\Phi_{j;i}(a)&:=&(\Phi_{i}\circ\cdots\circ\Phi_{j-1})(a),\\
\Phi_{i;}&:=&\Phi_{i;0}.\end{array}

The domains of these functions are assumed to be largest for which these
expressions make sense. We also write

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\sigma_{i;}&=&\sigma_{;i}&:=&\prod_{k<i}\varepsilon_{k}\iota_{k}\\
\sigma_{j;i}&=&\sigma_{i;j}&:=&\prod_{i\leqslant k<j}\varepsilon_{k}\iota_{k}\end{array}

We note that on their respective domains, the functions
\Phi_{i}
,
\Phi_{i;}
, and
\Phi_{j;i}
are strictly increasing if
\varepsilon_{i}\iota_{i}=1
,
\sigma_{i;}=1
, and
\sigma_{j;i}=1
, respectively, and strictly
decreasing in the contrary cases. We will write
\Phi_{;i}
and
\Phi_{i;j}
for the partial inverses of
\Phi_{i;}
and
\Phi_{j;i}
. We will also
use the abbreviations

\begin{array}[]{ccc}a_{i;}&:=&\Phi_{i;}(a)\\
a_{;i}&:=&\Phi_{;i}(a)\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{ccc}a_{j;i}&:=&\Phi_{j;i}(a)\\
a_{i;j}&:=&\Phi_{i;j}(a)\end{array}

For all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we set

\begin{array}[]{rclccc}L_{i}^{[1]}&:=&(\varphi_{i}-\sigma_{;i}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{i})_{i;}\\
L_{i}^{[2]}&:=&(\varphi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}-\varepsilon_{i}\sigma_{;i}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}})_{i;}\\
L_{i}^{[3]}&:=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\varnothing&\left.\text{if }\varphi_{i+1}=0\right.\\
&\text{or }\sigma_{;i+1}\varepsilon_{i+1}=-1\\
(\mathcal{L}_{\alpha_{i}}\varphi_{i+1})_{i+1;}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
L_{i}&:=&L_{i}^{[1]}\cup L_{i}^{[2]}\cup L_{i}^{[3]}.\end{array}

as well as

\begin{array}[]{rclccc}R_{i}^{[1]}&:=&(\varphi_{i}+\sigma_{;i}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{i})_{i;}\\
R_{i}^{[2]}&:=&(\varphi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\sigma_{;i}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}})_{i;}\\
R_{i}^{[3]}&:=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\varnothing&\left.\text{if }\varphi_{i+1}=0\right.\\
&\text{or }\sigma_{;i+1}\varepsilon_{i+1}=1\\
(\mathcal{L}_{\alpha_{i}}\varphi_{i+1})_{i+1;}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
R_{i}&:=&R_{i}^{[1]}\cup R_{i}^{[2]}\cup R_{i}^{[3]}\\
R&:=&\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}R_{i}.\end{array}

Note that

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle L_{i}^{[1]}=R_{i}^{[1]}=\varnothing\text{\quad and}

\displaystyle\psi_{i}=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle L_{i}^{[2]}=R_{i}^{[2]}=\varnothing.

The following lemma generalizes
[
5
, Lemma 8.1]
.

Lemma 6.3

If
a\in(L\mathrel{|}R)
, then
a_{;i}
is well defined for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Let us prove the lemma by induction on
i
. The result clearly holds for
i=0
. Assuming that
a_{;i}
is well defined, let
j>i
be minimal such
that
\varphi_{j}\neq 0
or
\psi_{j}\neq 0
. Note that we have
\alpha_{i}\geqslant\alpha_{i+1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\alpha_{j}
, so

E_{\alpha_{i}}\circ E_{\alpha_{i+1}}\circ\cdots\circ E_{\alpha_{j}}=E_{\gamma}
where
\gamma=\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+{\penalty}\alpha_{j}
. Applying
\Phi_{;i}
to the inequality

L_{j}<a<R_{j}\;,

we obtain

\sigma_{;i}(L_{j})_{;i}<\sigma_{;i}a_{;i}<\sigma_{;i}(R_{j})_{;i}.

Now if
\varphi_{j}\neq 0
, then

\displaystyle(L_{j})_{;i}

\displaystyle\supseteq

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}(E_{\gamma}(\varphi_{j}-\sigma_{;j}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j}))^{\iota_{i}}

\displaystyle(R_{j})_{;i}

\displaystyle\supseteq

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}(E_{\gamma}(\varphi_{j}+\sigma_{;j}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j}))^{\iota_{i}},

wso the number
z:=\sigma_{;i}\frac{a_{;i}-\varphi_{i}}{\varepsilon_{i}}
satisfies

\sigma_{;i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}(E_{\gamma}(\varphi_{j}-\sigma_{;j}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j}))^{\iota_{i}}<z<\sigma_{;i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}(E_{\gamma}(\varphi_{j}+\sigma_{;j}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j}))^{\iota_{i}}.

Both in the cases when
\sigma_{;i}=1
and when
\sigma_{;i}=-1
, it
follows that the number
((a_{;i}-\varphi_{i})/\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}})^{\iota_{i}}
is bounded from below by the hyperexponential

E_{\gamma}
of a number. Thus
a_{;j}=L_{\gamma}(((a_{;i}-\varphi_{i})/(\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}))^{\iota_{i}})
is well defined and so is
each
a_{;k}
for
i\leqslant k<j
. If
\varphi_{j}=0
, then we have

\psi_{j}\neq 0
and

\displaystyle(L_{j})_{;i}

\displaystyle\supseteq

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}(E_{\gamma}(\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{j}-\varepsilon_{j}\sigma_{;j}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\psi_{j}}))^{\iota_{i}},

\displaystyle(R_{j})_{;i}

\displaystyle\supseteq

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}(E_{\gamma}(\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}\sigma_{;j}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\psi_{j}}))^{\iota_{i}}.

Hence

\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}(E_{\gamma}(\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{j}-\varepsilon_{j}\sigma_{;j}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\psi_{j}}))^{\iota_{i}}<a_{;i}-\varphi_{i}<\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}(E_{\gamma}(\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}\sigma_{;j}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\psi_{j}}))^{\iota_{i}}

Both in the cases when
\varepsilon_{i}=1
and when
\varepsilon_{i}=-1
,
it follows that
((a_{;i}-\varphi_{i})/\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}})^{\iota_{i}}
is bounded from below by the hyperexponential

E_{\gamma}
of a number, so
a_{;j}
is well defined and so is each
a_{;k}
for
i\leqslant k<j
.
∎

6.2
Admissible sequences

Definition 6.4

Let
\Sigma:=(\varphi_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\psi_{i},\iota_{i},\alpha_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a coding
sequence and let
a\in\mathbf{No}
. We say that
a
is

\Sigma
-
admissible
if

a_{;i}
is well defined for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and

\displaystyle a_{;i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}(E_{\alpha_{i}}a_{;i+1})^{\iota_{i}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}

\displaystyle\succ

\displaystyle\log E_{\alpha_{i}}a_{;i+1},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\varphi_{i+1}

\displaystyle\vartriangleleft

\displaystyle\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{;i+1})\text{\hskip 10.22217ptif $\varphi_{i+1}\neq 0$}.

We say that
\Sigma
is
admissible
if there exists a
\Sigma
-admissible number.

Note that we do not ask that
\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}(E_{\alpha_{i}}a_{;i+1})^{\iota_{i}}
be a hyperserial expansion, nor even that
E_{\alpha_{i}}a_{;i+1}
be a monomial. For the rest of the section, we fix a coding sequence

\Sigma={(\varphi_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\psi_{i},\iota_{i},\alpha_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. We write
\mathbf{Ad}
for the class of

\Sigma
-admissible numbers. If
a\in\mathbf{Ad}
, then the definition of

\mathbf{Ad}
implicitly assumes that
a_{;i}
is well defined for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Note that if
\Sigma
is admissible, then so is
\Sigma_{\nearrow k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote by
\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k}
the corresponding class of
\Sigma_{\nearrow k}
-
admissible numbers.

The main result of this subsection is the following generalization
of
[
5
, Proposition 8.2]
:

Proposition 6.5

We have
\mathbf{Ad}=(L|R)
.

Proof.

Let
a\in\mathbf{Ad}\cup(L|R)
and let
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We have
a_{;i}\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
. If
\sigma_{;i}=1
, then
\Phi_{i;}
is
strictly increasing so we have

\displaystyle L_{i}^{[1]}<a<R_{i}^{[1]}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle(L_{i}^{[1]})_{\smash{;i}}<a_{;i}<(R_{i}^{[1]})_{\smash{;i}}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}-\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{i}<a_{;i}<\varphi_{i}+\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{i}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle a_{;i}-\varphi_{i}\prec\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{i}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}\trianglelefteqslant a_{;i}.

If
\sigma_{;i}=-1
, then
\Phi_{i;}
is strictly decreasing and
likewise we obtain
L_{i;}<a<R_{i;}\Longleftrightarrow\varphi_{i}\trianglelefteqslant a_{;i}
.

We have
\iota_{i}\log E_{\alpha_{i}}a_{;i+1}=\iota_{i}\left(\log\frac{a_{;i}-\varphi_{i}}{\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}}\right)
. If

\sigma_{;i}=1
, then
\Phi_{i;}
is strictly increasing so we have

\displaystyle L_{i}^{[2]}<a<R_{i}^{[2]}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}-\varepsilon_{i}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}}<a_{;i}<\varphi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle-\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}<\log\frac{a_{;i}-\varphi_{i}}{\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}}<\mathbb{R}^{>}\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}\succ\log\frac{a_{;i}-\varphi_{i}}{\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\log E_{\alpha_{i}}a_{;i+1}\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}.

Likewise, we have
L_{i}^{[2]}<a<R_{i}^{[2]}\Longleftrightarrow\log E_{\alpha_{i}}a_{;i+1}\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}
if
\sigma_{;i}=-1
.

Assume that
\varphi_{i+1}\neq 0
and
\sigma_{;i+1}=1
. If

\varepsilon_{i+1}=1
, then we have
a_{;i+1}>\varphi_{i+1}
.
Hence

\displaystyle L_{i}^{[3]}\cup L_{i+1}^{[1]}<a<R_{i}^{[3]}\cup R_{i+1}^{[1]}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\alpha_{i}}\varphi_{i+1}<a_{;i+1}\wedge\varphi_{i+1}\trianglelefteqslant a_{;i+1}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\varphi_{i+1}<\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{;i+1})\wedge\varphi_{i+1}\trianglelefteqslant a_{;i+1}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\varphi_{i+1}\vartriangleleft\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{;i+1}).

If
\varepsilon_{i+1}=-1
, then we have
a_{;i+1}>\varphi_{i+1}
, whence

\displaystyle L_{i}^{[3]}\cup L_{i+1}^{[1]}<a<R_{i}^{[3]}\cup R_{i+1}^{[1]}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle a_{;i+1}<\mathcal{L}_{\alpha_{i}}\varphi_{i+1}\wedge\varphi_{i+1}\trianglelefteqslant a_{;i+1}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{;i+1})<\varphi_{i+1}\wedge\varphi_{i+1}\trianglelefteqslant a_{;i+1}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\varphi_{i+1}\vartriangleleft\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{;i+1}).

Symmetric arguments apply when
\varphi_{i+1}\neq 0
and
\sigma_{;i+1}=-1
.

We deduce by definition of
\mathbf{Ad}
that
\mathbf{Ad}=\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}(L_{i}|R_{i})=(L|R)
.
∎

As a consequence of this last proposition and

[
5
, Proposition 4.29(a)]
, the class
\mathbf{Ad}
is a surreal
substructure if and only if
\Sigma
is admissible.

Example 6.6

Consider the coding sequence
\Sigma_{0}=(\varphi_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\iota_{i},\psi_{i},\alpha_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
where for all

i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L_{\omega^{2i}}\omega+L_{\omega^{2i}2}\omega+L_{\omega^{2i}3}\omega+\cdots,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1,

\displaystyle\psi_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L_{\omega^{2i+1}}\omega+L_{\omega^{2i+1}2}\omega+L_{\omega^{2i+1}3}\omega+\cdots,

\displaystyle\iota_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-1\text{\quad and}

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{2i+1}.

We use the notations from Section
6.1
. We
claim that
\Sigma_{0}
is admissible. Indeed for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, set

a_{i}:=\varphi_{0}+\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{0}}\left(E_{\omega}^{\varphi_{1}+\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{1}}\left(E_{\small{\omega^{3}}}^{{\mathinner{\mskip 1.0mu\raise 1.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{.}}\mskip 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{.}\mskip 2.0mu\raise 7.0pt\hbox{.}\mskip 1.0mu}}^{\varphi_{i}}}\right)^{-1}}\right)^{-1}.

Given
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
i>j
, we have
L_{j}<a_{i}
and
a_{i}<R_{j}
.
We deduce that
L<R
, whence
\Sigma_{0}
is admissible.

Lemma 6.7

Let
a\in\mathbf{Ad}
and
b\in\mathbf{No}
be
such that
a-\varphi_{0}
and
b-\varphi_{0}
have the same sign and the
same dominant monomial. Then
b\in\mathbf{Ad}
.

Proof.

For
x,y\in\mathbf{No}^{\neq}
, we write
x\equiv y
if
x\asymp y
and

x
and
y
have the same sign. Let us prove by induction on
i\in\mathbb{N}
that
b_{;i}
is defined and that
a_{;i}-\varphi_{i}\equiv b_{;i}-\varphi_{i}
. Since this implies that
\varphi_{i}\trianglelefteqslant b_{;i}
, that
\log\frac{b_{;i}-\varphi_{i}}{\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}}\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}
, and
that
\varphi_{i}\vartriangleleft\sharp_{\alpha_{i-1}}(b_{;i})
if
i>0
, this will yield
b\in\mathbf{Ad}
.

The result follows from our hypothesis if
i=0
. Assume now that
a_{;i}-\varphi_{i}\equiv b_{;i}-\varphi_{i}
and let us prove that
a_{;i+1}-\varphi_{i+1}\equiv b_{;i+1}-\varphi_{i+1}
. Let

c_{i}:=\left(\frac{b_{;i}-\varphi_{i}}{\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}}\right).

We have
c_{i}\equiv\left(\frac{a_{;i}-\varphi_{i}}{\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}}\right)^{\iota_{i}}=E_{\alpha_{i}}a_{;i+1}\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
, so
b_{;i+1}=L_{\alpha_{i}}(c_{i})
is defined.
Moreover
c_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{i}}[E_{\alpha_{i}}a_{;i+1}]
so

b_{;i+1}\in\mathcal{L}_{\alpha_{i}}[a_{;i+1}]
. Since
\varphi_{i+1}\vartriangleleft\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{;i+1})=\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(b_{;i+1})
, we deduce that
{b_{;i+1}-\varphi_{i+1}}\sim a_{;i+1}-\varphi_{i+1}
, whence in particular
b_{;i+1}-\varphi_{i+1}\equiv a_{;i+1}-\varphi_{i+1}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 6.8

We have
\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}=\mathcal{L}_{\alpha_{0}}[\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}]
.

Proof.

For
b\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}
, and
c\in\mathcal{L}_{\alpha_{0}}[b]
, we have
\varphi_{1}\vartriangleleft\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(b)=\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(c)
so
c-\varphi_{1}\sim b-\varphi_{1}
. We conclude
with the previous lemma.
∎

Lemma 6.9

For
a,b\in\mathbf{Ad}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}^{>}
, we have
L_{\alpha_{i-1}}a_{;i}<\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{i-1}}b_{;i}
.

Proof.

Let
j>i
be minimal with
\varphi_{j}\neq 0
or
\psi_{j}\neq 0
. We thus
have
a_{;j},b_{;j}\in\mathcal{P}[\varphi_{j}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{j}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{j}}]
so
\log a_{;j}\prec b_{;j}
. We have
a_{;i}=E_{\alpha_{i}+\cdots+\alpha_{j-1}}a_{;j}
and
b_{;i}=E_{\alpha_{i}+\cdots+\alpha_{j-1}}b_{;j}
where
\alpha_{i}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\alpha_{j}\geqslant 1
. We deduce by induction using
Lemma
5.27
that
L_{\alpha_{i-1}}a_{;i}<\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{i-1}}b_{;i}
.
∎

6.3
Nested sequences

In this subsection, we assume that
\Sigma
is admissible. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
we say that a
\Sigma_{\nearrow k}
-admissible number
a
is

\Sigma_{\nearrow k}
-nested
if we have

E_{\alpha_{k+i}}a_{k;i+1}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{k+i}}\setminus L_{<\alpha_{k+i}}\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{k+i}\omega}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We write
\mathbf{Ne}_{\nearrow k}
for the
class of
\Sigma_{\nearrow k}
-nested numbers. For
k=0
we simply say that

a
is
\Sigma
-nested and we write
\mathbf{Ne}:=\mathbf{Ne}_{\nearrow 0}
.

Definition 6.10

We say that
\Sigma
is

nested
if for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k}=\varphi_{k}+\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k+1})^{\iota_{k}}.

Note that the inclusion
\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k}\subseteq\varphi_{k}+\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k+1})^{\iota_{k}}
always holds. In
[
5
, Section 8.4]
, we gave examples
of nested and admissible non-nested sequences in the case of transseries, i.e.
with
\alpha_{i}=1
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We next give an example in the
hyperserial case.

Example 6.11

We claim that the sequence
\Sigma_{0}
from
Example
6.6
is nested. Indeed, let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
a\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k+1}
. We have
a=\varphi_{k+1}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}(E_{\omega^{2k+3}}b)^{-1}
for a certain
b\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
with
b\asymp L_{\smash{\omega^{2k+4}}}\omega
.
Let us check that the conditions of Definition
6.4

are satisfied for
c:=\varphi_{k}+\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k}}(E_{\omega^{2k+1}}a)^{-1}
.

First let
\mathfrak{m}\in\operatorname{supp}\psi_{k}
. We want to prove that

\mathfrak{m}\succ\log E_{\omega^{2k+1}}a
. We have
\mathfrak{m}=L_{\smash{\omega^{2k+1}}n}\omega
for a certain
n\in\mathbb{N}^{>}
.
Now
a<2L_{\smash{\omega^{2k+2}}}\omega
, so
\log E_{\omega^{2k+1}}a\prec E_{\omega^{2k+1}2}^{L_{\smash{\omega^{\small{2k+2}}}}\omega}=L_{\smash{\omega^{2k+2}}}(\omega+2)\prec\mathfrak{m}
.

Secondly, let
\mathfrak{n}\in\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{k}
. We want to prove
that
\mathfrak{n}\succ\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k}}(E_{\omega^{2k+1}}a)^{-1}
.
We have
\mathfrak{n}=L_{\smash{\omega^{2k}}n}\omega
for a certain
n\in\mathbb{N}^{>}
. Then
\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k}}(E_{\omega^{2k+1}}a)^{-1}\prec\mathrm{e}^{2\psi_{k}}
by the previous paragraph. Now
2\psi_{k}+\mathbb{N}<3L_{\omega^{2k+1}}\omega
so
\mathrm{e}^{2\psi_{k}}\prec\mathrm{e}^{3L_{\omega^{2k+1}}\omega}\prec\mathfrak{n}
.

Finally, we claim that
\varphi_{k+1}\vartriangleleft\sharp_{\omega^{2k+1}}(a)
. This is immediate since the dominant term
\tau
of

\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}(E_{\omega^{2k+3}}b)^{-1}
is positive
infinite, so
\varphi_{k+1}\vartriangleleft\varphi_{k+1}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\tau\trianglelefteqslant\sharp_{\omega^{2k+1}}(a)
. Therefore
\Sigma_{0}
is
nested.

A crucial feature of nested sequences is that they are sufficient to describe
nested expansions. This is the content of Theorem
6.15

below.

Lemma 6.12

Let
b\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}
. If

\alpha_{0}>1
, or
\alpha_{0}=1
and
b_{\succ}
is not tail-atomic, then
the hyperserial expansion of
E_{\alpha_{0}}\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(b)
is

E_{\alpha_{0}}\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(b)=E_{\alpha_{0}}^{\smash{\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(b)}}

If
\alpha_{0}=1
,
b_{\succ}=\psi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\iota\mathfrak{b}
is
tail-atomic, and
\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{b}}=L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{u}
is a
hyperserial expansion, then
\psi\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}
and the
hyperserial expansion of
\exp b_{\succ}
is

\exp b_{\succ}=\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{u})^{\iota}.

Proof.

Recall that
\sharp_{1}(b)=b_{\succ}
. By Corollary
6.8
,
we have
\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(b)\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}
,. So we may
assume without loss of generality that
b=\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(b)
.

We claim that
E_{\alpha_{0}}^{b}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}}\setminus L_{<\alpha_{0}}\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}\omega}
. Assume for contradiction that

E_{\alpha_{0}}^{b}\in L_{<\alpha_{0}}\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}\omega}
and write

E_{\alpha_{0}}^{b}=L_{\gamma}\mathfrak{a}
accordingly. Then
Corollary
5.6
implies that
\gamma=0
, in which
case we define
n:=0
, or
\alpha_{0}=\omega^{\mu+1}
for some
ordinal
\mu
and
\gamma=(\alpha_{0})_{/\omega}n
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}^{>}
. Therefore
E_{\alpha_{0}}^{b+n}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}\omega}
, so
{b+n}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}\omega}
. This implies that

b=(b+n)\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}(-n).

Recall that
\varphi_{1}\vartriangleleft b
. Assume that
n=0
, so

\varphi_{1}=0
. Since
b
is
\log
-atomic, we also have
\psi_{1}=0
. Let

j>1
be minimal with
\varphi_{j}\neq 0
or
\psi_{j}\neq 0
. We have

\alpha_{1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\alpha_{j-1}
and
b_{1;j}=L_{\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{j-1}}b\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{j-1}\omega}
. In particular, the number
b_{1;j}
is log-atomic. If
\varphi_{j}\neq 0
, this contradicts the fact that
\varphi_{j}\vartriangleleft b_{1;j}
. If
\psi_{j}\neq 0
, then
\operatorname{supp}\psi_{j}\succ\log((b_{1;j}\mathrm{e}^{-\psi_{j}})^{\iota_{j}})
implies

\log b_{1;j}=\psi_{j}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\log((b_{1;j}\mathrm{e}^{-\psi_{j}})^{\iota_{j}}).

But then
\log b_{1;j}
is not a monomial: a contradiction. Assume now
that
n>0
. So
\varphi_{1}=b+n
and
b=\varphi_{1}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}(-n)
. But
then
b_{1;2}
is not defined: a contradiction. We conclude that

E_{\alpha_{0}}^{b}\not\in L_{<\alpha_{0}}\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}\omega}
.

If
\alpha_{0}>1
, or if
\alpha_{0}=1
and
b
is not tail-atomic, then our
claim yields the result. Assume now that
\alpha_{0}=1
and that
b=\psi\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\iota\mathfrak{b}
is tail-atomic where
\iota\in\{-1,1\},\psi\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ}
, and
\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{b}}=L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{u}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\omega}
is a hyperserial expansion. Then the
hyperserial expansion of
\exp b
is
\exp b=\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{u})^{\iota}
.

We next show that
\psi\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}
. If
\mathfrak{b}\not\in\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{1}}(E_{\alpha_{1}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 2})^{\iota_{1}}
,
then
\varphi_{1}\vartriangleleft\psi
, and we conclude with
Lemma
6.7
that
\psi\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}
. Assume
for contradiction that
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{1}}(E_{\alpha_{1}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 2})^{\iota_{1}}
. Since
\mathfrak{b}
is log-atomic, we
must have
\psi_{1}=0
. By the definition of coding sequences, this implies
that
\iota_{1}=1
and
\alpha_{1}=1
. So
b=\varphi_{1}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{1}\exp(b_{1;2})
, whence
\psi=\varphi_{1}
,
\iota=\varepsilon_{1}
, and
\mathfrak{b}=\exp(b_{1;2})
. In particular the
number
b_{1;2}
is
\log
-
atomic, hence tail-atomic. Since
b_{1;2}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 2}
, the claim in the second paragraph of the
proof, applied to
\Sigma_{\nearrow 1}
, gives
E_{1}^{b_{1;2}}\not\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\omega}
. But then also
\mathfrak{b}\not\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\omega}
: a contradiction.
∎

We pursue with two auxiliary results that will be used order to construct a
infinite path required in the proof of Theorem
6.15

below.

Lemma 6.13

For
a\in\mathbf{Ad}
, there is a finite path

P
in
a
with
u_{P,|P|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}-\mathbb{N}
or

\psi_{P,|P|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}-\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.16
, it is enough to find such a path in

E_{\alpha_{0}}a_{;1}
. Write
\alpha_{0}=:\omega^{\mu}
. Assume
first that
\mu=0
, so
\alpha_{0}=1
and
\psi_{0}=0
. If
(a_{;1})_{\succ}
is not tail-atomic, then the hyperserial expansion of
\exp(a_{;1})_{\succ}
is
\exp(a_{;1})_{\succ}=E_{1}^{\smash{(a_{;1})_{\succ}}}
and
rE_{1}^{\smash{(a_{;1})_{\succ}}}
is the dominant term
of
\exp a_{;1}
for some
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
. Then the path
P
with

|P|=1
and
\tau_{P,0}:=rE_{1}^{\smash{(a_{;1})_{\succ}}}

satisfies
u_{P,|P|}=(a_{;1})_{\succ}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}
.
If
(a_{;1})_{\succ}
is tail-atomic, then there exist
\psi\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}
,
\iota\in\{-1,1\}
and
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\omega}
such that the hyperserial expansion of
\exp(a_{;1})_{\succ}
is
\exp(a_{;1})_{\succ}=\mathrm{e}^{\psi}\mathfrak{a}^{\iota}
. Let
r\mathrm{e}^{\psi}\mathfrak{a}^{\iota}
be a term
in
\exp a_{;1}
with
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
. Then the path
P
with
|P|=1
and
P(0):=r\mathrm{e}^{\psi}\mathfrak{a}^{\iota}
satisfies

\psi_{P,|P|}=\psi\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}-\mathbb{N}
.

Assume now that
\mu>0
. In view of (
12
), we recall
that there are an ordinal
\lambda<\alpha_{0}
and a number
\delta
with

E_{\alpha_{0}}a_{;1}=E_{\lambda}\left(L_{\lambda}E_{\alpha_{0}}^{\smash{\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(a_{;1})}}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta\right).

If
\mu
is a limit ordinal, then by Lemma
6.12
,
we have a hyperserial expansion
