\mathfrak{m}:=L_{\lambda}E_{\alpha_{0}}^{\smash{\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(a_{;1})}}
. Let
\tau\in\operatorname{term}\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(a_{;1})
and set
Q(0)=\mathfrak{m}
and

Q(1):=\tau
, so that
Q
is a path in
\mathfrak{m}
. By
Lemma
5.15
, there is a subpath in
E_{\alpha_{0}}a_{;1}
, hence also a path
P
in
E_{\alpha_{0}}a_{;1}
, with
\tau_{P,|P|-1}=\mathfrak{m}
. So
u_{P,|P|}=\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(a_{;1})\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}
. If
\mu
is a successor ordinal, then we may
choose
\lambda=\omega^{\mu_{-}}n
for a certain
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By
Lemma
6.12
, we have a hyperserial expansion

\mathfrak{m}:=E_{\alpha_{0}}^{\smash{\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(a_{;1})-n}}
. As in the previous case, there is a path
P
in
E_{\alpha_{0}}a_{;1}

with
\tau_{P,|P|}=\mathfrak{m}
, whence
u_{P,|P|}=\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(a_{;1})-n\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}-\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Corollary 6.14

For
a\in\mathbf{Ad}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
there is a finite path
P
in
a
with
|P|\geqslant k
and
u_{P,|P|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k}-\mathbb{N}
or
\psi_{P,|P|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k}-\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

This is immediate if
k=0
. Assume that the result holds at
k
and pick a
corresponding path
P
with
u_{P,|P|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k}-\mathbb{N}
(resp.
\psi_{P,|P|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k}-\mathbb{N}
). Note that the dominant term
\tau
of
u_{P,|P|}-\varphi_{k}
(resp.
\psi_{P,|P|}-\varphi_{k}
) lies in
\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k+1})^{\iota_{k}}
by
Lemma
6.7
. Moreover
\tau
is a term of
u_{P,|P|}
(resp.
\psi_{P,|P|}
). By the previous lemma, there is a path
Q
in

\tau
with
u_{Q,|Q|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k+1}-\mathbb{N}
or

\psi_{Q,|Q|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k+1}-\mathbb{N}
, so
(P(0),\ldots,P(|P|-1),Q(0))\ast Q
satisfies the conditions.
∎

Theorem 6.15

There is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\Sigma_{\nearrow k}
is nested.

Proof.

Assume for contradiction that this is not the case. This means that the set

\Delta
of indices
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d}\neq\varphi_{d}+\varepsilon_{d}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}(E_{\alpha_{d}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d+1})^{\iota_{d}}
is infinite. We
write
\Delta=\{d_{i}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
where
d_{0}<d_{1}<\cdots
. Fix
a\in\mathbf{Ad}
and let
d:=d_{i}\in\Delta
. Let
u\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d+1}
such that

\varphi_{d}+\varepsilon_{d}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}(E_{\alpha_{d}}u)^{\iota_{d}}\not\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d},

(49)

let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
P
be any finite path with

u_{P,|P|}=\varphi_{d}+\varepsilon_{d}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}(E_{\alpha_{d}}u)^{\iota_{d}}-n.

We claim that we can extend
P
to a path
Q
with
|Q|>|P|
,
u_{Q,|Q|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d_{i+3}}-\mathbb{N}
and such that
|P|

is a bad index in
Q
. Indeed, in view of
Definition

6.4
for

\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d}
, the relation (
49
) translates into
the following three possibilities:

\bullet

There is an
\mathfrak{n}\in\operatorname{supp}\psi_{d}
with
\mathfrak{n}\preccurlyeq\log E_{\alpha_{d}}u
. We then have
\log E_{\alpha_{d}}a_{;d+1}\prec\mathfrak{n}\preccurlyeq\log E_{\alpha_{d}}u
. By
Lemma
6.7
and the convexity of
\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d+1}
, we deduce that
\iota_{d}(\psi_{d})_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathfrak{n}
lies
in
\iota_{d}\log E_{\alpha_{d}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d+1}
, so

\mathrm{e}^{(\psi_{d})_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathfrak{n}}\in(E_{\alpha_{d}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d+1})^{\iota_{d}}
. By
Corollary
6.14
for the admissible sequence starting
with
(0,1,0,\iota_{d},\alpha_{d})
and followed by
\Sigma_{\nearrow d+1}
, there is a finite path
R_{0}
in
\mathrm{e}^{(\psi_{d})_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathfrak{n}}
with
|R_{0}|\geqslant d_{i+3}-d>2
and
u_{R_{0},|R_{0}|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d_{i+3}}-\mathbb{N}
. Taking the
logarithm and using Lemma
5.14
, we obtain a finite
path
R_{1}
in
(\psi_{d})_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathfrak{n}
, hence in
\psi_{d}
,
with
|R_{1}|\geqslant 2
and
u_{R_{1},|R_{1}|}=u_{R_{0},|R_{0}|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d_{i+3}}-\mathbb{N}
. Write
(E_{\alpha_{d}}a_{;d+1})^{\iota_{d}}=r\mathfrak{m}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\rho
where
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}
and
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbf{Mo}^{\neq}
. Then
\log\mathfrak{m}\asymp E_{\alpha_{d}}a_{;d+1}\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi_{d}
, so
the hyperserial expansion of
\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}\mathfrak{m}
has one of the
following forms

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta}(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{u})^{\iota}\text{\quad or}

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(E_{1}^{\psi_{d}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\delta})^{\iota}

where
(L_{\beta}E_{\alpha}^{u})^{\iota}
is a hyperserial expansion and

\delta
is purely large. In both cases, the path
R=(\varepsilon_{d}r\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}\mathfrak{m})\ast R_{1}
is a finite path
R
in

\varepsilon_{d}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}(E_{\alpha_{d}}a_{;d+1})^{\iota_{d}}
with

u_{R,|R|}=u_{R_{1},|R_{1}|}\in{\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d_{i+3}}-\mathbb{N}}
. Since
R(0)
is a term in
u_{P,|P|}
, we may consider
the path
Q:=P\ast R
. Moreover, since
\tau_{Q,|P|}
is a term
in
\psi_{d}=\psi_{Q,|P|}
, the index
|P|
is bad for
Q
.

\bullet

We have
\log E_{\alpha_{d}}u\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi_{d}
, but there is
an
\mathfrak{m}\in\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{d}
with
\mathfrak{m}\preccurlyeq\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}(E_{\alpha_{d}}u)^{\iota_{d}}
. We then have

\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}(E_{\alpha_{d}}a_{;d+1})^{\iota_{d}}\prec\varphi_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathfrak{m}\preccurlyeq\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}(E_{\alpha_{d}}u)^{\iota_{d}}
. By Lemma
6.7
and the
convexity of
\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d+1}
, we deduce that

(\varphi_{d})_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathfrak{m}
lies in
\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}(E_{\alpha_{d}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d+1})^{\iota_{d}}
. So
L_{\alpha_{d}}((\mathrm{e}^{-\psi_{d}}(\varphi_{d})_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathfrak{m})^{\iota_{d}})

lies in
\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d+1}
. But then also
v:=\sharp_{\alpha_{d}}(L_{\alpha_{d}}((\mathrm{e}^{-\psi_{d}}(\varphi_{d})_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathfrak{m})^{\iota_{d}}))
lies in

\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d+1}
by Corollary
6.8
. By
Corollary
6.14
, there is a finite path
R_{0}
in
v

with
|R_{0}|>2
and
u_{R_{0},|R_{0}|}\in{\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d_{i+3}}-\mathbb{N}}
. Applying Lemma
5.15
to this path

R_{0}
in
v
, we obtain is a finite path
R_{1}
in
(\mathrm{e}^{-\psi_{d}}(\varphi_{d})_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathfrak{m})^{\iota_{d}}
with
u_{R_{1},|R_{1}|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d_{i+3}}-\mathbb{N}
. Since

(\varphi_{d})_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}(E_{\alpha_{d}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d+1})^{\iota_{d}}
, we have

\operatorname{supp}\psi_{d}\succ\mathrm{e}^{-\psi_{d}}(\varphi_{d})_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathfrak{m}
. So Lemma
5.16
implies that there is a
finite path
R
in
(\varphi_{d})_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathfrak{m}
, hence in

\varphi_{d}
, with
u_{R,|R|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d_{i+3}}-\mathbb{N}
. We have
R(0)\in\operatorname{term}\varphi_{d}\setminus\mathbb{R}\subseteq\operatorname{term}u_{P,|P|}
, so
Q:=P\ast R
is
a path. Write
\tau
for the dominant term of
\varepsilon_{d}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}(E_{\alpha_{d}}u^{\prime})^{\iota_{d}}
. The index
|P|
is a bad
in
Q
because
\tau_{Q,|P|}
and
\tau
both lie in
\operatorname{term}a_{Q,|P|}
, and
\tau_{Q,|P|}\succ\tau
.

\bullet

We have
\log E_{\alpha_{d}}u\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi_{d}
and

\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{d}\succ\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d}}(E_{\alpha_{d}}u)^{\iota_{d}}
,
but
\varphi_{d+1}=\sharp_{\alpha_{d}}(\varphi_{d+1}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{d+1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d+1}}(E_{\alpha_{d+1}}u)^{\iota_{d+1}})
. By the definition of
\alpha_{d}
-truncated numbers,
there is a
\beta<\alpha_{d}
with

\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d+1}}(E_{\alpha_{d+1}}u)^{\iota_{d+1}}\prec\frac{1}{L_{\beta}E_{\alpha_{d}}^{\varphi_{d+1}}}\prec\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{d+1}}(E_{\alpha_{d+1}}a_{;d+2})^{\iota_{d+1}}.

By convexity of
\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d+2}
, we get

L_{\beta}E_{\alpha_{d}}^{\varphi_{d+1}}\in\mathrm{e}^{-\psi_{d+1}}(E_{\alpha_{d+1}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d+2})^{-\iota_{d+1}}
. By
similar arguments as above (using Corollary
6.14
and
Lemmas
5.15
and
5.14
), we
deduce that there is a finite path
R
in
\varphi_{d+1}
with
u_{R,|R|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d_{i+2}}-\mathbb{N}
. As in the previous
case
Q:=P\ast R
is a path and
|P|
is a bad index in
Q
.

Consider a
b\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d_{1}-1}
and the path
P_{0}:=\left(\tau_{a-\varphi_{d_{0}}}\right)
in
b
. So
P
is a finite path
with
u_{P_{0},|P_{0}|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d_{1}}
. Thus there exists a
path
P_{1}
which extends
P_{0}
with
u_{P_{1},|P_{1}|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d_{3}}
, where
|P_{0}|
is a bad index in
P
.
Repeating this process iteratively for
i=2,3,\ldots
, we construct a
path
P_{i}
that extends
P_{i-1}
and such that
u_{P_{i},|P_{i}|}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow d_{2i+1}}
and such that
|P_{i-1}|
is a bad
index in
P_{i}
. At the limit, this yields an infinite path
Q
in
a
that
extends each of the paths
P_{i}
. This path
Q
has a cofinal set of bad
indices, which contradicts Theorem
1.1
. We conclude that
there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\Sigma_{\nearrow k}
is nested.
∎

Lemma 6.16

If
\Sigma
is nested, then

\mathbf{Ad}=\varphi_{0}+\varepsilon_{0}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{0}}(\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{0}}[E_{\alpha_{0}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}])^{\iota_{0}}
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{0}}[E_{\alpha_{0}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}]=E_{\alpha_{0}}\mathcal{L}_{\alpha_{0}}[\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}]
. The result
thus follows from Corollary
6.8
and the assumption that

\Sigma
is nested.
∎

Lemma 6.17

Assume that
\Sigma
is nested. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
a\in\mathbf{Ad}
and
c_{k}\in\mathbf{No}
with

c_{k}=\varphi_{k}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k}}\mathfrak{p}^{\iota_{k}}

(50)

for a certain
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbf{Mo}^{\succcurlyeq}
with

\mathfrak{p}\sqsubseteq E_{\alpha_{j}}a_{;k+1}
and
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[E_{\alpha_{k}}a_{;k+1}]
whenever
\psi_{k}=0
. If

c_{k}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k}
, then we have

(c_{k})_{k;}\sqsubseteq a.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
5.30
. We
have
a_{;k}=\varphi_{k}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}a_{;k+1})^{\iota_{k}}
and we must have
\operatorname{supp}\psi_{k}\succ\log\mathfrak{p}
since
c_{k}=\varphi_{k}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k}}\mathfrak{p}^{\iota_{k}}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k}
. If
follows from the deconstruction lemmas in
Section

5.3

that
c_{k}\sqsubseteq a_{;k}
. This proves the result in the case when
k=0
.

Now assume that
k>0
. Setting
c_{k-p}:=\Phi_{k-p;k}(c_{k})
,
let us prove by induction on
p\leqslant k
that

\displaystyle c_{k-p}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k-p}

\displaystyle c_{k-p}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{k-p-1}}

\displaystyle c_{k-p}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq

\displaystyle a_{;k-p}.

For
p=k
, the last relation yields the desired result.

If
p=0
, then we have
c_{k}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k}
by assumption
and we have shown above that
c_{k}\sqsubseteq a_{;k}
. We have
\varphi_{k}\vartriangleleft\sharp_{\alpha_{k-1}}(c_{k})
and
\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k}}\mathfrak{p}^{\iota_{j}}
is a monomial, so (
50
) yields
c_{k}=\sharp_{\alpha_{k-1}}(c_{k})\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{k-1}}
.
This deals with the case
p=0
. In addition, we have
c_{k}>0
because

k>0
and
c_{k}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow k}
. Let us show that

\log c_{k}\prec a_{;k}.

(51)

If
\varphi_{k}\neq 0
, then this follows from the facts that
\varphi_{k}\vartriangleleft a_{;k}
and
\varphi_{k}\vartriangleleft c_{k}
. If

\varphi_{k}=0
and
\psi_{k}\neq 0
, then
\log(c_{k}/\varepsilon_{k})\sim\psi_{k}\sim\log(a_{;k}/\varepsilon_{k})\prec a_{;k}
. If
\varphi_{k}=\psi_{k}=0
, then
a_{;k}=E_{\alpha_{k}}a_{;k+1}
and
c_{k}=\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\omega}[a_{;k}]
, so
\log c_{k}\prec a_{;k}
.

Assume now that
0<p\leqslant k
and that the induction hypothesis holds
for all smaller
p
. We have

c_{k-p}=\Phi_{k-p}(c_{k-p+1})=\varphi_{k}+\varepsilon_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k}}\left(E_{\alpha_{k-p}}^{\smash{c_{k-p+1}}}\right)^{\iota_{k}}

(52)

Since
\Sigma
is nested, we immediately obtain
\varphi_{k-p}\vartriangleleft\sharp_{\alpha_{k-p-1}}(c_{k-p})
, whence
c_{k-p}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{k-p-1}}
as above. Since
c_{k-p-1}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow(k-p-1)}
and
\Sigma
is nested, we have

c_{k-p}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow(k-p)}
.
Using (
52
), (
51
), and the decomposition lemmas, we
observe that the relation
c_{k-p}\sqsubseteq a_{;k-p}
is equivalent
to

E_{\alpha_{k-p}}^{\smash{c_{k-p+1}}}\sqsubseteq E_{\alpha_{k-p}}a_{;k-p+1}.

(53)

We have
c_{k-p+1}\sqsubseteq a_{;k-p+1}
, so
c_{k-p+1}\sqsubseteq\sharp_{\alpha_{k-p}}(a_{;k-p+1})
. Note that

E_{\alpha_{k-p}}^{\smash{\sharp_{\alpha_{k-p}}(a_{;k-p+1})}}=\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha_{k-p}}(E_{\alpha_{k-p}}a_{;k-p+1})\sqsubseteq E_{\alpha_{k-p}}a_{;k-p+1}.

So it is enough, in order to derive
Equation

53
, to prove that

E_{\alpha_{k-p}}^{\smash{c_{k-p+1}}}\sqsubseteq E_{\alpha_{k-p}}^{\smash{\sharp_{\alpha_{k-p}}(a_{;k-p+1})}}
. Now

L_{\alpha_{k-p}}c_{k-p+1}<\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{k-p}}\sharp_{\alpha_{k-p}}(a_{;k-p+1})

by Lemma
6.9
, whence
E_{\alpha_{k-p}}^{\smash{c_{k-p+1}}}\sqsubseteq E_{\alpha_{k-p}}^{\smash{\sharp_{\alpha_{k-p}}(a_{;k-p+1})}}
by Lemma
5.25
.
∎

For
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
g\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{i}}
and
a\in\mathbf{Ad}
, we have
\varphi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}g(E_{\alpha_{i}}a_{;i+1})^{\iota_{i}}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow i}
by
Lemma
6.16
. We may thus consider the strictly
increasing bijection

\Psi_{i,g}:=\mathbf{Ad}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Ad};a\longmapsto(\varphi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}g(E_{\alpha_{i}}a_{;i+1})^{\iota_{i}})_{i;}.

We will prove Theorem
1.2
by proving that the function
group
\mathcal{G}:=\{\Psi_{i,g}:i\in\mathbb{N},g\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{i}}\}
on
\mathbf{Ad}
generates the class
\mathbf{Ne}
,
i.e. that we have
\mathbf{Ne}=\mathbf{Smp}_{\mathcal{G}}
. We first need
the following inequality:

Lemma 6.18

Assume that
\Sigma
is nested. Let
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
with
i<j
and let
g\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{i}}
. On

\mathbf{Ad}
, we have
\Psi_{i,g}<\Psi_{j,H_{2}}
if
\sigma_{j+1;i+1}=1
and
\Psi_{i,g}<\Psi_{j,H_{1/2}}
if
\sigma_{j+1;i+1}=-1
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove the result for
j=i+1
. Assume that
\sigma_{i+2;i+1}=1
. Let
a\in\mathbf{Ad}
and set
a^{\prime}:=(\Psi_{i+1,H_{2}}(a))_{i+1;}
, so that

\displaystyle a_{;i+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{i+1}+\varepsilon_{i+1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i+1}}(E_{\alpha_{i+1}}a_{;i+2})^{\iota_{i+1}}

\displaystyle a^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{i+1}+\varepsilon_{i+1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i+1}}(2E_{\alpha_{i+1}}a_{;i+2})^{\iota_{i+1}}.

Note that

\displaystyle(\Psi_{i,g}(a))_{i+1;}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\alpha_{i}}[a_{;i+1}].

If
\sigma_{;i+1}=1
, then
\varepsilon_{i+1}\iota_{i+1}=\sigma_{;i+2}/\sigma_{;i+1}=1
and
\Psi_{;i+1}
is strictly
increasing. So we only need to prove that
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha_{i}}[a_{;i+1}]<a^{\prime}
, which reduces to proving that
\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{;i+1})<\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a^{\prime})
. Let
\tau
be the dominant term of
E_{\alpha_{i+1}}a_{;i+2}
. Our assumption that
\Sigma
is nested gives
\varphi_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i}}(E_{\alpha_{i}}a^{\prime})^{\iota_{i}}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow i}
, whence
\varphi_{i+1}\vartriangleleft\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a^{\prime})
. We deduce that
\varphi_{i+1}+\varepsilon_{i+1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i+1}}(2\tau)^{\iota_{i+1}}\trianglelefteqslant\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a^{\prime})
.
Lemma

5.26
implies that

\varphi_{i+1}+\varepsilon_{i+1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i+1}}(2\tau)^{\iota_{i+1}}
is
\alpha_{i}
-truncated.

\displaystyle\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{;i+1})-\varphi_{i+1}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i+1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i+1}}\tau^{\iota_{i+1}},

\displaystyle\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a^{\prime})-\varphi_{i+1}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i+1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i+1}}(2\tau)^{\iota_{i+1}}

and
\varepsilon_{i+1}\iota_{i+1}=1
implies that

\varepsilon_{i+1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i+1}}(2\tau)^{\iota_{i+1}}-\varepsilon_{i+1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{i+1}}\tau^{\iota_{i+1}}

is a strictly positive term. We deduce that
\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{;i+1})-\varphi_{i+1}<\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a^{\prime})-\varphi_{i+1}
, whence

\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{;i+1})<\sharp_{\alpha_{i}}(a^{\prime})
. The other cases
when
\sigma_{;i+1}=-1
or when
\sigma_{i+2;i+1}=-1
are
proved similarly, using symmetric arguments.
∎

We are now in a position to prove the following refinement of

Theorem

1.2
.

Theorem 6.19

If
\Sigma
is nested, then
\mathbf{Ne}
is a surreal
substructure with
\mathbf{Ne}=\mathbf{Smp}_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.1
, the class

\mathbf{Smp}_{\mathcal{G}}
is a surreal substructure, so it is enough to
prove the equality. We first prove that
\mathbf{Smp}_{\mathcal{G}}\subseteq\mathbf{Ne}
.

Assume for contradiction that there are an
a\in\mathbf{Smp}_{\mathcal{G}}
and a
k\in\mathbb{N}
, which we choose
minimal, such that
a_{;k}
cannot be written as
a_{;k}=\varphi_{k}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{k}\mathfrak{m}_{k}
where
\mathfrak{m}_{k}=\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k}}(E_{\alpha_{k}}^{a_{;k+1}})^{\iota_{k}}
is a hyperserial expansion. Set

\mathfrak{m}:=\mathfrak{d}_{a_{;k}-\varphi_{k}}
,
r:=(a_{;k})_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\delta:=(a_{;k})_{\succ\mathfrak{m}}
.

Our goal is to prove that there is a number
m\in\{k,k+1\}
and

\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbf{Mo}^{\succ}
with

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\mathfrak{p}&\in&\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{m}}[E_{\alpha_{m}}a_{;m+1}]\\
\mathfrak{p}&\sqsubseteq&E_{\alpha_{m}}a_{;m+1}\\
\mathfrak{p}&\sqsubset&E_{\alpha_{m}}a_{;m+1},\quad\text{whenever $\delta=0$ and $r\in\{-1,1\}$.}\end{array}

(54)

Assume that this is proved and set
c_{m}:=\varphi_{m}+\varepsilon_{m}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{m}}\mathfrak{p}^{\iota_{m}}
. The first condition and
Lemma
6.16
yield
c_{m}\in\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow m}

and the relations
\log\mathfrak{p}\prec\operatorname{supp}\psi_{m}
and

\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{m}}\mathfrak{p}^{\iota_{m}}\prec\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{m}
. The
second and third condition, together with Lemma
6.17
, imply

c:=(c_{m})_{m;}\sqsubset a
. The first condition also implies that

c\in\mathcal{G}[a]
: a contradiction. Proving the existence of
m

and
\mathfrak{p}
is therefore sufficient.

If
\mathfrak{m}\neq\min\operatorname{supp}a_{;k}
or
\mathfrak{m}=\min\operatorname{supp}a_{;k}
and
r\not\in\{-1,1\}
, then
m:=k
and

\mathfrak{p}:=\mathfrak{d}_{E_{\alpha_{k}}a_{;k+1}}

satisfy (
54
). Assume now that
\mathfrak{m}=\min\operatorname{supp}a_{;k}
and that
r\in\{-1,1\}
, whence
r=\varepsilon_{k}
. If
a_{;k+1}\not\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{k}}
then
m:=k
and

\mathfrak{p}:=E_{\alpha_{k}}^{\smash{\sharp_{\alpha_{k}}(a_{;k+1})}}
satisfy (
54
). Assume therefore that
a_{;k+1}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{k}}
. This implies that there exist
\gamma<\alpha_{k}
and
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{k}\omega}
with

E_{\alpha_{k}}^{\smash{a_{;k+1}}}=L_{\gamma}\mathfrak{a}.
By the
definition of coding sequences, there is a least index
j>k
with

\varphi_{j}\neq 0
or
\psi_{j}\neq 0
, so

E_{\alpha_{k}}^{\smash{a_{;k+1}}}=E_{\alpha_{k}+\cdots+\alpha_{j-1}}\left(\varphi_{j}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{j}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{j}}\left(E_{\alpha_{j}}^{\smash{a_{;j+1}}}\right)^{\iota_{j}}\right)\not\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{k}\omega}.

We have
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{k}\omega}
and
L_{\gamma}\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{k}}\setminus\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{k}\omega}
. So by
Corollary

5.6
, we must have
\alpha_{k}=\omega^{\mu+1}
for a certain
\mu\in\mathbf{On}
and
\gamma=(\alpha_{k})_{/\omega}n
for a certain
n\in\mathbb{N}^{>}
. Note
that
a_{;k+1}=L_{\alpha_{k}}\mathfrak{a}-n
. Recall that
\varphi_{k+1}\vartriangleleft a_{;k+1}
and
L_{\alpha_{k}}\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbf{Mo}^{\succ}
, so
\varphi_{k+1}\in\{L_{\alpha_{k}}\mathfrak{a},0\}
. The case
\varphi_{k+1}=L_{\alpha_{k}}\mathfrak{a}
cannot occur
for otherwise

a_{;k+2}=\left(\frac{a_{;k+1}-\varphi_{k+1}}{\varepsilon_{k+1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}}\right)^{\iota_{k+1}}=\frac{n^{\iota_{k+1}}}{\varepsilon_{k+1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}}

would not lie in
\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
. So
\varphi_{k+1}=0
. Let
m:=k+1
and

\mathfrak{p}:=\left(\frac{L_{\alpha_{k}}\mathfrak{a}}{\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}}\right)^{\iota_{k+1}}=\left(\frac{\mathfrak{d}_{a_{;k+1}}}{\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{k+1}}}\right)^{\iota_{k+1}}=\mathfrak{d}_{E_{\alpha_{k+1}}a_{;k+2}}.

We have
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{k+1}}[E_{\alpha_{k+1}}a_{;k+2}]
and
\mathfrak{p}\sqsubset E_{\alpha_{k+1}}a_{;k+2}
,
so
m
and
\mathfrak{p}
satisfy (
54
). We deduce that

\mathbf{Smp}_{\mathcal{G}}
is a subclass of
\mathbf{Ne}
.

Conversely, consider
b\in\mathbf{Ne}
and set
c:=\pi_{\mathcal{G}}[b]
. So there are
i_{1},i_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
and
(g,h)\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{i_{1}}}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{i_{2}}}^{\prime}
with

\Psi_{i_{1},g_{1}}(b)<c<\Psi_{i_{2},g_{2}}(b)
. Let
i:=\max(i_{1}+1,i_{2}+1)
. By Lemma
6.18
, there exist
d_{1},d_{2}\in\left\{1/2,2\right\}
with
\Psi_{i_{1},g_{1}}<\Psi_{i,H_{d_{1}}}
and

\Psi_{i_{2},g_{2}}<\Psi_{i,H_{d_{2}}}
, whence
\Psi_{i,H_{d_{1}^{-1}}}(b)<c<\Psi_{i,H_{d_{2}}}(b)
. Since
\Phi_{;i}
is strictly monotonous, we
get
c_{;i}-\varphi_{i}\asymp b_{;i}-\varphi_{i}
. The numbers

\varepsilon_{i}(c_{;i}-\varphi_{i})
and
\varepsilon_{i}(b_{;i}-\varphi_{i})
are monomials, so
c_{;i}-\varphi_{i}=b_{;i}-\varphi_{i}
.
Therefore
b=c\in\mathbf{Smp}_{\mathcal{G}}
.
∎

In view of Theorem
6.19
, Lemma
6.18
, and
Proposition
4.1
, we have the following
parametrization of
\mathbf{Ne}
:

\forall z\in\mathbf{No},\quad\Xi_{\mathbf{Ne}}z=\{L,\Psi_{\mathbb{N},\mathcal{H}}\Xi_{\mathbf{Ne}}z_{L}|\Psi_{\mathbb{N},\mathcal{H}}\Xi_{\mathbf{Ne}}z_{R},R\}.

We conclude this section with a few remarkable identities for

\Xi_{\mathbf{Ne}}
.

Lemma 6.20

If
\Sigma
is nested, then for
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
a,b\in\mathbf{Ne}
, we have
a\sqsubseteq b\Longleftrightarrow a_{;i}\sqsubseteq b_{;i}
.

Proof.

By
[
5
, Lemma 4.5]
and since the function
\Phi_{i;}
is
strictly monotonous, it is enough to prove that
\forall a,b\in\mathbf{Ne},a\sqsubseteq b\Longleftarrow a_{;i}\sqsubseteq b_{;i}
. By
induction, we may also restrict to the case when
i=1
. So assume that

a_{;1}\sqsubseteq b_{;1}
. Recall that
L_{\alpha_{0}}a_{;1}\prec\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{0}}b_{;1}
by Lemma
6.9
. Since

a_{;1},b_{;1}\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{0}}
, we deduce with
Lemma
5.25
that
E_{\alpha_{0}}^{\smash{a_{;1}}}\sqsubseteq E_{\alpha_{0}}^{\smash{b_{;1}}}
. It follows using the decomposition lemmas
that
a\sqsubseteq b
.
∎

Proposition 6.21

If
\Sigma
is nested, then
\mathbf{Ne}=(\mathbf{Ne}_{\nearrow 1})_{1;}=\varphi_{0}+\varepsilon_{0}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{0}}(E_{\alpha_{0}}^{\mathbf{Ne}_{\nearrow 1}})^{\iota_{0}}
.

Proof.

We have
\mathbf{Ne}\subseteq(\mathbf{Ne}_{\nearrow 1})_{1;}
by
definition of
\mathbf{Ne}
. So we only need to prove that

(\mathbf{Ne}_{\nearrow 1})_{1;}\subseteq\mathbf{Ne}
. Consider
b\in\mathbf{Ne}_{\nearrow 1}
. Since
\Sigma
is nested, the number
a:=\varphi_{0}+\varepsilon_{0}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{0}}(E_{\alpha_{0}}b)
is

\Sigma
-admissible, so we need only justify that
E_{\alpha_{0}}b\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}}\setminus L_{<\alpha_{0}}\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}\omega}
. Since
a
is
\Sigma
-admissible, we have
\varphi_{1}\vartriangleleft\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(b)
. But
b
is
\Sigma_{\nearrow 1}
-nested, so
b=\varphi_{1}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\tau
for a certain term
\tau
. We
deduce that
b=\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(b)\in\mathbf{No}_{\succ,\alpha_{0}}
,
whence
E_{\alpha_{0}}b\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}}
.

Assume for contradiction that
E_{\alpha_{0}}^{b}\in L_{<\alpha_{0}}\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}\omega}
and write
E_{\alpha_{0}}^{b}=L_{\gamma}\mathfrak{a}
where
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}\omega}
and

\gamma<\alpha_{0}
. Note that
\gamma\neq 0
: otherwise
\varphi_{i}
and

\psi_{i}
would be zero for all
i\geqslant 1
, thereby contradicting
Definition
6.1
(
e
). By
Corollary
5.6
, we must have
\alpha_{0}=\omega^{\mu+1}
for a certain ordinal
\mu
and
\gamma=\omega^{\mu}n
for a certain

n\in\mathbb{N}^{>}
. Consequently,
b=L_{\alpha_{0}}\mathfrak{a}-n\in\mathbf{Mo}-n
. If
\varphi_{1}\neq 0
, then the condition
\varphi_{1}\vartriangleleft\sharp_{\alpha_{0}}(b)
implies
\varphi_{1}=b
, which
leads to the contradiction that
b_{1;2}=0\not\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
.
If
\varphi_{1}=0
, then
\mathbf{Ne}_{\nearrow 1}\subseteq\varepsilon_{1}\mathbf{Mo}
, whence
n=0
: a contradiction.
∎

Corollary 6.22

If
\Sigma
is nested, then for
z\in\mathbf{No}
, we have

\Xi_{\mathbf{Ne}}z=\varphi_{0}+\varepsilon_{0}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{0}}\left(E_{\alpha_{0}}^{\smash{\Xi_{\mathbf{Ne}_{\nearrow 1}}\sigma_{;1}z}}\right)^{\iota_{0}}.

Corollary 6.23

If
\Sigma
is nested and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\Xi_{\mathbf{Ne}}=\Phi_{k;}\circ\Xi_{\mathbf{Ne}_{\nearrow k}}\circ H_{\sigma_{;k}}.

Proposition 6.24

Assume that
\Sigma
is nested with
(\varphi_{0},\varepsilon_{0},\psi_{0},\iota_{0})=(0,1,0,1)
, assume that
\alpha_{0}\in\omega^{\mathbf{On}+1}
and write
\beta:=(\alpha_{0})_{/\omega}
. Consider the coding
sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
with
(\varphi^{\prime}_{i},\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i},\psi^{\prime}_{i},\iota^{\prime}_{i},\alpha^{\prime}_{i})=(\varphi_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\psi_{i},\iota_{i},\alpha_{i})
for all

i\in\mathbb{N}
, with the only exception that

\varphi_{1}^{\prime}=\varphi_{1}-n.

If
\psi_{1}<0
, or
\psi_{1}=0
and
\iota_{1}=-1
, then
\Sigma^{\prime}
is
nested and we have

\Xi_{\smash{\mathbf{Ne}^{\prime}}}=L_{\beta n}\circ\Xi_{\mathbf{Ne}},

where
\mathbf{Ne}^{\prime}
is the class of
\Sigma^{[n]}
-nested numbers.

Proof.

Assume that
\psi_{1}<0
, or
\psi_{1}=0
and
\iota_{1}=-1
. In
particular, if
a
is
\Sigma
-admissible, then
a_{;1}-\varphi_{1}\prec 1
, so
a_{;1}-\varphi_{1}\prec\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{1}^{\prime}
. For
b\in\mathbf{No}^{>,\succ}
, it follows that
E_{\alpha_{0}}(b-n)
is

\Sigma^{[n]}
-admissible if and only if
E_{\alpha_{0}}b
is

\Sigma
-admissible. Let
\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow i}^{\prime}
be the class of

\Sigma^{\prime}_{\nearrow i}
-
admissible numbers, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We have
L_{\beta n}\mathbf{Ad}=\mathbf{Ad}^{\prime}
by the
previous remarks, and
\Sigma^{\prime}
is admissible. For
i>1
, we have

\Sigma^{\prime}_{\nearrow i}=\Sigma_{\nearrow i}
, so

\mathbf{Ad}^{\prime}_{\nearrow i}=\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow i}\supseteq\varphi^{\prime}_{i}+\varepsilon^{\prime}_{i}\mathrm{e}^{\psi^{\prime}_{i}}(E_{\alpha^{\prime}_{i}}\mathbf{Ad}^{\prime}_{\nearrow i+1})^{\iota^{\prime}_{i}}.

Moreover,
\mathbf{Ad}^{\prime}_{\nearrow 1}=\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}-n
, so

\displaystyle\mathbf{Ad}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\supseteq

\displaystyle L_{\beta n}\mathbf{Ad}\supseteq L_{\beta n}E_{\alpha_{0}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 1}=L_{\beta n}E_{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbf{Ad}^{\prime}_{\nearrow 1}+n)=E_{\alpha_{0}^{[n]}}\mathbf{Ad}^{\prime}_{\nearrow 1}

\displaystyle\mathbf{Ad}^{\prime}_{\nearrow 1}

\displaystyle\supseteq

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}-n+\varepsilon_{1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi_{1}}(E_{\alpha_{1}}\mathbf{Ad}_{\nearrow 2})^{\iota_{1}}=\varphi^{\prime}_{1}+\varepsilon^{\prime}_{1}\mathrm{e}^{\psi^{\prime}_{1}}(E_{\alpha_{1}^{[n]}}\mathbf{Ad}^{\prime}_{\nearrow 2})^{\iota^{\prime}_{1}}.

So
\Sigma^{\prime}
is nested. We deduce that
L_{\beta n}\mathbf{Ne}=\mathbf{Ne}^{\prime}
, that is, we have a strictly increasing bijection
L_{\beta n}:\mathbf{Ne}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Ne}^{\prime}
. It is enough to prove that for

a,b\in\mathbf{Ne}
with
a\sqsubseteq b
, we have
L_{\beta n}a\sqsubseteq L_{\beta n}b
. Proceeding by induction on
n
, we may assume
without loss of generality that
n=1
. By
[
6
, identity
(6.3)]
, the function
L_{\beta}
has the following equation on

\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}}
:

\forall\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}},\quad L_{\beta}\mathfrak{a}=\left\{L_{\beta}\mathfrak{a}_{L}^{\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}}}|L_{\beta}\mathfrak{a}_{R}^{\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}}},\mathfrak{a}\right\}_{\mathbf{Mo}_{\alpha_{0}}}.

So it is enough to prove that
L_{\beta}b<a
. Note that
L_{\beta}b=E_{\alpha_{0}}^{b_{;1}-1}
and
a=E_{\alpha_{0}}^{a_{;1}}
where
b_{;1}-\varphi_{1},a_{;1}-\varphi_{1}\prec 1
. So
b_{;1}-a_{;1}\prec 1
,
whence
b_{;1}-1<a_{;1}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

6.4
Pre-nested and nested numbers

Let
a\in\mathbf{No}
be a number. We say that
a
is
pre-nested
if
there exists an infinite path
P
in
a
without any bad index for
a
. In
that case,
Lemma

6.2
yields a coding sequence
\Sigma_{P}
which
is admissible due to the fact that
a\in(L|R)
with the notations from

Section

6
. By Theorem
6.15
, we get a
smallest
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(\Sigma_{P})_{\nearrow k}
is nested. If

k=0
, then we say that
a
is
nested
. In that case,
Theorem
6.19
ensures that the class
\mathbf{Ne}
of
\Sigma_{P}
-nested
numbers forms a surreal substructure, so
a
can uniquely be written as
a=\Xi_{\mathbf{Ne}}(c)
for some surreal parameter
c\in\mathbf{No}
.

One may wonder whether it could happen that
k>0
. In other words: do there
exist pre-nested numbers that are not nested? For this, let us now describe an
example of an admissible sequence
\Sigma^{\ast}
such that the class

\mathbf{Ne}_{\Sigma^{\ast}}
of
\Sigma^{\ast}
-nested numbers contains
a smallest element
b
. This number
b
is pre-nested, but cannot be nested by
Theorem
6.19
. Note that our example is “transserial” in the sense that
it does not involve any hyperexponentials.

Example 6.25

Let
\Sigma=(\varphi_{i},\varepsilon_{i},0,1,1)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a nested sequence with
\varepsilon_{1}=-1
. Let
a
be
the simplest
\Sigma
-nested number. We define a coding sequence

\Sigma^{\ast}=(\varphi_{i}^{\ast},\varepsilon_{i}^{\ast},0,1,1)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
by

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}^{\ast}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-1

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}^{\ast}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\varphi_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{e}^{a_{;2}}}

\displaystyle(\varphi_{i}^{\ast},\varepsilon_{i}^{\ast})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(\varphi_{i},\varepsilon_{i})\text{\qquad for all $i>0$.}

Note that

a_{;1}=\varphi_{1}-\mathrm{e}^{a_{;2}}=\varphi_{1}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{1}\mathrm{e}^{a_{;2}},

where
\mathrm{e}^{a_{;2}}
is an infinite monomial, so
b:=\varphi_{0}^{\ast}-\mathrm{e}^{a_{;1}}
is
\Sigma^{\ast}
-nested. In
particular, the sequence
\Sigma^{\ast}
is admissible.

Assume for contradiction that there is a
\Sigma^{\ast}
-nested number
c

with
c<b
. Since
\varepsilon_{0}^{\ast}=\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}=-1
,
we have
c_{;2}<b_{;2}
. Recall that
c_{;2}
and
b_{;2}
are purely
large, so
\mathrm{e}^{c_{;2}}\prec\mathrm{e}^{b_{;2}}=\mathrm{e}^{a_{;2}}
. In
particular

\mathrm{e}^{c_{;1}}=\mathrm{e}^{\varphi_{1}-\mathrm{e}^{c_{;2}}}\succcurlyeq\mathrm{e}^{\varphi_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{e}^{a_{;2}}}=\varphi_{0}^{\ast},

which contradicts the assumption that
c
is
\Sigma^{\ast}
-nested. We
deduce that
b
is the minimum of the class
\mathbf{Ne}_{\Sigma^{\ast}}
of

\Sigma^{\ast}
-nested numbers. In view of Theorem
6.19
, the sequence

\Sigma^{\ast}
cannot be nested.

The above examples shows that there exist admissible sequences that are not
nested. Let us now construct an admissible sequence
\Sigma^{\varnothing}

such that the class
\mathbf{Ne}_{\Sigma^{\varnothing}}
of

\Sigma^{\varnothing}
-nested numbers is actually empty.

Example 6.26

We use the same notations as in
Example

6.25
. Set

(\varphi_{0}^{\varnothing},\varepsilon_{0}^{\varnothing}):={(\mathrm{e}^{b},1)}
and
(\varphi_{i}^{\varnothing},\varepsilon_{i}^{\varnothing}):=(\varphi_{i-1}^{\ast},\varepsilon_{i-1}^{\ast})
for all
i>0
. We
claim that the coding sequence
\Sigma^{\varnothing}:={(\varphi_{i}^{\varnothing},\varepsilon_{i}^{\varnothing},0,1,1)}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is admissible. In order to see this, let
\psi:=1/2\mathrm{e}^{b_{;1}}
. Then

\mathrm{e}^{\varphi_{1}^{\varnothing}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{1}\psi}=\mathrm{e}^{\varphi_{0}^{\ast}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{0}^{\ast}\psi}\prec\mathrm{e}^{\varphi_{0}^{\ast}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{0}^{\ast}\mathrm{e}^{b_{;1}}}=\mathrm{e}^{b}.

Since
\varphi_{1}^{\varnothing}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{1}\psi
is

(\Sigma^{\varnothing})_{\nearrow 1}
-admissible (i.e.

\Sigma^{\ast}
-admissible), we deduce that
\mathrm{e}^{b}+\mathrm{e}^{\varphi_{1}^{\varnothing}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\varepsilon_{1}\psi}
is

\Sigma^{\varnothing}
-admissible, whence
\Sigma^{\varnothing}
is
admissible. Assume for contradiction that

\mathbf{Ne}_{\Sigma^{\varnothing}}
is non-empty, and let
\mathrm{e}^{b}\mathbin{+\!\!\!\!+}\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbf{Ne}_{\Sigma^{\varnothing}}
. Then
\log\mathfrak{m}
is
\Sigma^{\ast}
-nested, so
\log\mathfrak{m}\geqslant b
,
whence
\mathfrak{m}\succcurlyeq\mathrm{e}^{b}
: a contradiction.

7
Numbers as hyperseries

Traditional transseries in
x
can be regarded as infinite expressions that
involve
x
, real constants, infinite summation, exponentiation and
logarithms. It is convenient to regard such expressions as infinite labeled
trees. In this section, we show that surreal numbers can be represented
similarly as infinite expressions in
\omega
that also involve
hyperexponentials and hyperlogarithms. One technical difficulty is that the
most straightforward way to do this leads to ambiguities in the case of nested
numbers. These ambiguities can be resolved by associating a surreal number to
every infinite path in the tree. In view of the results from

Section

6
, this will enable us to regard any surreal number
as a unique hyperseries in
\omega
.

Remark 7.1

In the case of ordinary transseries, our notion of tree expansions below is
slightly different from the notion of tree representations that was used
in
[
30
,
38
]
. Nevertheless, both notions coincide modulo
straightforward rewritings.

7.1
Introductory example

Let us consider the monomial
\mathfrak{m}=\exp\left(2E_{\omega}\omega-\sqrt{\omega}+L_{\omega+1}\omega\right)
from Example
5.3
.
We may recursively expand
\mathfrak{m}
as

\mathfrak{m}=\mathrm{e}^{2E_{\omega^{\small{2}}}^{L_{\omega^{\small{2}}}\omega+1}-E_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}L_{1}\omega}}(L_{\omega}\omega).

In order to formalize the general recursive expansion process, it is more
convenient to work with the unsimplified version of this expression

\mathfrak{m}=\mathrm{e}^{2\cdot\mathrm{e}^{0}\cdot\left(E_{\omega^{\small{2}}}^{1\cdot\mathrm{e}^{0}\cdot\left(L_{\omega^{\small{2}}}\omega\right)^{1}+1\cdot 1}\right)^{1}+(-1)\cdot\mathrm{e}^{{}_{0}}\cdot\left(E_{1}^{1/2\cdot\mathrm{e}^{0}\cdot(L_{1}\omega)^{1}}\right)^{1}}(L_{\omega}\omega)^{1}.

Introducing
\wp_{c}:x\longmapsto x^{c}
as a notation for the “power”
operator, the above expression may naturally be rewritten as a tree:

In the next subsection, we will describe a general procedure to expand surreal
monomials and numbers as trees.

7.2
Tree expansions

In what follows, a
tree

T
is a set of nodes
N_{T}
together with a
function that associates to each node
\nu\in N_{T}
an
arity

\ell_{\nu}\in\mathbf{On}
and a sequence
(\nu[\alpha])_{\alpha<\ell_{\nu}}\in N_{T}^{\ell_{\nu}}
of
children
; we write
C_{\nu}:=\{\nu[\alpha]:\alpha<\ell_{\nu}\}
for the set of
children of
\nu
. Moreover, we assume that
N_{T}
contains a special element

\rho_{T}
, called the
root
of
T
, such that for any
\nu\in N_{T}

there exist a unique
h
(called the
height
of
\nu
and also denoted
by
h_{\nu}
) and unique nodes
\nu_{0},\ldots,\nu_{h}
with
\nu_{0}=\rho_{T}
,

\nu_{h}=\nu
, and
\nu_{i}\in C_{\nu_{i-1}}
for
i=1,\ldots,h
. The
height
h_{T}
of the tree
T
is the maximum of the heights of all nodes; we
set
h_{T}:=\omega
if there exist nodes of arbitrarily large heights.

Given a class
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, an
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-labeled
tree
is a tree together with a map
\lambda:N_{T}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{\Sigma};\nu\longmapsto\lambda_{\nu}
, called the

labeling
. Our final objective is to express numbers using

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-labeled trees, where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{\neq}\cup\left\{\omega,\sum,\times,\wp_{-1},\wp_{1}\right\}\cup L_{\omega^{\mathbf{On}}}\cup E_{\omega^{\mathbf{On}}}.

Instead of computing such expressions in a top-down manner (from the leaves
until the root), we will compute them in a bottom-up fashion (from the root
until the leaves). For this purpose, it is convenient to introduce a separate
formal symbol
\textrm{?}_{c}
for every
c\in\mathbf{On}
, together with the
extended signature

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\sharp}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\cup\left\{\textrm{?}_{c}:c\in\mathbf{No}\right\}.

We use
\textrm{?}_{c}
as a placeholder for a tree expression for
c
whose
determination is postponed to a later stage.

Consider a
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\sharp}
-labeled tree
T
and a map
v:N_{T}\longrightarrow\mathbf{On}
. We say that
v
is an
evaluation
of
T

if for each node
\nu\in N_{T}
one of the following statements holds:

E1

\lambda_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{\neq}\cup\{\omega\}
,
\ell_{\nu}=0
, and
v(\nu)=\lambda_{\nu}
;

E2

\lambda_{\nu}=\sum
, the family
(v(\nu[\alpha]))_{\alpha<\ell_{\nu}}
is well based and
v(\nu)=\sum_{\alpha<\ell_{v}}v(\nu[\alpha])
;

E3

\lambda_{\nu}=\times
,
\ell_{\nu}=2
, and
v(\nu)=v(\nu[0])v(\nu[1])
;

E4

\lambda_{\nu}=\wp_{\iota}
,
\iota\in\{-1,1\}
,

\ell_{\nu}=1
, and
v(\nu)=v(\nu[0])^{\iota}
;

E5

\lambda_{\nu}=L_{\omega^{\mu}}
,
\ell_{\nu}=1
, and
v(\nu)=L_{\omega^{\mu}}v(\nu[0])
;

E6

\lambda_{\nu}=E_{\omega^{\mu}}
,
\ell_{\nu}=1
, and
v(\nu)=E_{\omega^{\mu}}v(\nu[0])
;

E7

\lambda_{\nu}=\textrm{?}_{\alpha}
,
\ell_{\nu}=0
,
and
v(\nu)=\alpha
.

We call
v(\rho_{T})
the
value
of
T
via
v
. We say that
a\in\mathbf{No}
is
a value
of
T
if there exists an evaluation of
T

with
a=v(\rho_{T})
.

Lemma 7.2

a)

If
T
has finite height, then there
exists at most one evaluation of
T
.

b)

Let
v
and
v^{\prime}
be evaluations of
T
with
v(\rho_{T})=v^{\prime}(\rho_{T})
. Then
v=v^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This is straightforward, by applying the rules

\mathbf{E1}
–
\mathbf{E7}
recursively (from the
leaves to the root in the case of (
a
) and
the other way around for (
b
)).
∎

Although evaluations with a given end-value are unique for a fixed tree
T
,
different trees may produce the same value. Our next aim is to describe a
standard way to expand numbers using trees. Let us first consider the case of
a monomial
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbf{Mo}
. If
\mathfrak{m}=1
, then the

standard monomial expansion
of
\mathfrak{m}
is the

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\sharp}
-labeled tree
T
with
N_{T}=\{\rho_{T}\}
and

\lambda_{\rho_{T}}=1
. Otherwise, we may write
\mathfrak{m}=\mathrm{e}^{\psi}(L_{\beta}g)^{\iota}
with
g=\omega
or
g=E_{\alpha}^{u}
. Depending on
whether
g=\omega
or
g=E_{\alpha}^{u}
, we respectively take

T:=\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\includegraphics[width,height]{hynotex-2.pdf}}\qquad\text{ or }\qquad T:=\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\includegraphics[width,height]{hynotex-3.pdf}}

and call
T
the
standard monomial expansion
of
\mathfrak{m}
. Let
us next consider a general number
a\in\mathbf{No}
and let
\ell\in\mathbf{On}
be the ordinal size of its support. Then we may write
a=\sum_{\alpha<\ell}c_{\alpha}\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}
for a sequence

(c_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\ell}\in(\mathbb{R}^{\neq})^{\ell}
and
a
\prec
-
decreasing sequence
(\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\ell}\in\mathbf{Mo}^{\ell}
. For each
\alpha<\ell
, let
T_{\alpha}
be the
standard monomial expansion of
\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}
. Then we define the

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\sharp}
-labeled tree

T:=\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\includegraphics[width,height]{hynotex-4.pdf}}

and call it the
standard expansion
of
a
. Note that the height of

T
is at most
6
, there exists a unique evaluation
v:N_{T}\longrightarrow\mathbf{No}
of
T
, and
v(\rho_{T})=a
.

Now consider two trees
T
and
T^{\prime}
with respective labelings
\lambda:N_{T}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\sharp}
and
\lambda^{\prime}:N_{T^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\sharp}
. We say that
T^{\prime}

refines

T
if
N_{T^{\prime}}\supseteq N_{T}
and there exist evaluations
v:N_{T}\longrightarrow\mathbf{No}
and
v^{\prime}:N_{T^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{No}
such that
v(\nu)=v^{\prime}(\nu)
for all
\nu\in N_{T}
and

\lambda_{\nu}=\lambda^{\prime}_{\nu}
whenever
\lambda_{\nu}\not\in\textrm{?}_{\mathbf{No}}
. Now assume that
v(\rho_{T})=a
for some evaluation
v:N_{T}\longrightarrow\mathbf{No}
. Then we say that
T
is a
tree
expansion
of
a
if for every
\nu\in N_{T}
with
\lambda_{\nu}=\sum
, the subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
with root
\nu
refines the standard
expansion of
v(\nu)
. In particular, a tree expansion
T
of a number
a\in\mathbf{No}
with
\lambda_{\rho_{T}}\not\in\textrm{?}_{\mathbf{No}}
always refines
the standard expansion of
a
.

Lemma 7.3

Any
a\in\mathbf{No}
has a unique tree
expansion with labels in
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
.

Proof.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that an

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\sharp}
-labeled tree
T
is
n
-settled
if

\lambda_{\nu}\not\in\textrm{?}_{\mathbf{No}}
for all nodes
\nu\in N_{T}
of
height
<n
. Let us show how to construct a sequence
(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\sharp}
-labeled tree expansions of
a

such that the following statements hold for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

S1

T_{n}
is an
n
-settled and of finite height;

S2

v_{n}(\rho_{T_{n}})=a
for some (necessarily
unique) evaluation
v_{n}:N_{T_{n}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{No}
of
T_{n}
;

S3

If
n>0
, then
T_{n}
refines
T_{n-1}
;

S4

If
T
is a tree expansion of
a
with labels in

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, then
T
refines
T_{n}
.

We will write
\lambda_{n}:N_{T_{n}}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\sharp}
for the labeling of
T_{n}
.

We take
T_{0}
such that
N_{T_{0}}=\{\rho_{T_{0}}\}
and

\lambda_{\rho_{T_{0}}}=\textrm{?}_{a}
. Setting
v_{0}(\rho_{T_{0}}):=a
, the
conditions
\mathbf{S1}
–
\mathbf{S4}
are naturally
satisfied.

Assume now that
T_{n}
has been constructed and let us show how to construct

T_{n+1}
. Let
S
be the subset of
N_{T_{n}}
of nodes
\nu
of level
n

with
v_{n}(\nu)\in\textrm{?}_{\mathbf{No}}
. Given
\nu\in S
, let
T_{\nu}

be the standard expansion of
v_{n}(\nu)
and let
v_{\nu}
be the unique
evaluation of
T_{\nu}
. We define
T_{n+1}
to be the tree that is
obtained from
T_{n}
when replacing each node
\nu\in S
by the
tree
T_{\nu}
.

Since each tree
T_{\nu}
is of height at most
6
, the height of
T_{n+1}
is finite. Since
T_{n+1}
is clearly
(n+1)
-settled, this proves

\mathbf{S1}
. We define an evaluation
v_{n+1}:N_{T_{n+1}}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\sharp}
by setting
v_{n+1}(\sigma)={\penalty}v_{n}(\sigma)
for any
\sigma\in N_{T_{n}}
and
v_{n+1}(\sigma)=v_{\nu}(\sigma)
for any
\nu\in S
and
\sigma\in N_{T_{\nu}}
(note that
v_{n+1}
is well defined since
v_{\nu}(\rho_{T_{\nu}})=(\lambda_{n})_{\nu}={\penalty}v_{n}(\nu)
for all
\nu\in S
). We have
v_{n+1}(\rho_{T_{n+1}})=v_{n}(\rho_{T_{n}})=a
, so

\mathbf{S2}
holds for
v_{n+1}
. By construction,
N_{T_{n+1}}\supseteq N_{T_{n}}
and the evaluations
v_{n}
and
v_{n+1}
coincide
on
N_{T_{n}}
; this proves
\mathbf{S3}
. Finally, let
T
be a
tree expansion of
a
with labels in
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
and let
v
be the
unique evaluation of
T
with
v(\rho_{T})=a
. Then
T
refines
T_{n}
, so

v
coincides with
v_{n}
on
N_{T_{n}}
. Let
\nu\in S
. Since
T
is a tree
expansion of
a
, the subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
with root
\nu
refines
T_{\nu}
,
whence
N_{T}\supseteq N_{T_{\nu}}
. Moreover,
v(\nu)=v_{n+1}(\nu)
,
so
v
coincides with
v_{\nu}
on
T_{\nu}
. Altogether, this shows that

T
refines
T_{n+1}
.

Having completed the construction of our sequence, we next define a

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-labeled tree
T_{\infty}
and a map
v_{\infty}:N_{T_{\infty}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{No}
by taking
N_{T_{\infty}}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}N_{T_{n}}
and by setting
(\lambda_{\infty})_{\nu}:=(\lambda_{n})_{\nu}
and
v_{\infty}(\nu)=v_{n}(\nu)
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\nu\in N_{T_{n}}
such that
(\lambda_{n})_{\nu}\not\in\textrm{?}_{\mathbf{No}}
. By construction, we have
v_{\infty}(\rho_{T_{\infty}})=a
and
T_{\infty}
refines
T_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

We claim that
T_{\infty}
is a tree expansion of
a
. Indeed, consider a
node
\nu\in N_{T_{\infty}}
of height
n
with
\lambda_{\nu}=\sum
. Then
\nu\in N_{T_{n+1}}
and
(\lambda_{n+1})_{\nu}=\sum
, since
T_{n+1}
is
(n+1)
-settled. Consequently, the
subtree of
T_{n+1}
with root
\nu
refines the standard expansion of

v_{n+1}(\nu)
. Since
T_{\infty}
refines
T_{n+1}
, it follows that
the subtree of
T_{\infty}
with root
\nu
also refines the standard
expansion of
v_{\infty}(\nu)=v_{n+1}(\nu)
. This completes the proof
of our claim.

It remains to show that
T_{\infty}
is the unique tree expansion of
a

with labels in
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. So let
T
be any tree expansion of
a

with labeling
\lambda:N_{T}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. For every

n\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows from
\mathbf{S4}
that
N_{T}\supseteq N_{T_{n}}
. Moreover, since
T_{n}
is
n
-settled,
\lambda

coincides with both
\lambda_{n}
and
\lambda_{\infty}
on those nodes in

N_{T_{n}}
that are of height
<n
. Consequently,
N_{T}\supseteq N_{T_{\infty}}
and
\lambda
coincides with
\lambda_{\infty}

on
N_{T_{\infty}}
. Since every node in
N_{T}
has finite height, we
conclude that
T=T_{\infty}
.
∎

7.3
Hyperserial descriptions

From now on, we only consider tree expansions with labels in

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, as in
Lemma

7.3
. Given a class

\mathbf{Ne}
of nested numbers as in
Section

6
, it can be
verified that every element in
\mathbf{Ne}
has the same tree expansion. We
still need a notational way to distinguish numbers with the same expansion.

Let
a\in\mathbf{No}
be a pre-nested number. By
Theorem
6.15
, we get a smallest
k\in\mathbb{N}
such
that
(\Sigma_{P})_{\nearrow k}
is nested. Hence
a_{P,k}\in\mathbf{Ne}
for
the class
\mathbf{Ne}
of
(\Sigma_{P})_{\nearrow k}
-nested numbers.

Theorem

6.19
implies that there exists a unique number
c
with
a_{P,k}=\Xi_{\mathbf{Ne}}(\sigma_{;k}c)
. We call
c
the
nested rank

of
a
and write
\xi_{a}:=c
. By
Corollary

6.23
, we note that

\xi_{u_{P,i}}=\sigma_{;i}\xi_{a}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Given an
arbitrary infinite path
P
in a number
a\in\mathbf{No}
, there exists a
k>0
such that
P_{\nearrow k}
has no bad indices for
a_{P,k}
(modulo a
further increase of
k
, we may even assume
a_{P,k}
to be nested). Let

\sigma_{P,k}:=\operatorname{sign}(r_{P,0}\cdots r_{P,k-1})\iota_{P,0}\cdots\iota_{P,k-1}\in\{-1,1\}
. We call
\xi_{P}:=\sigma_{P,k}\xi_{u_{P,k}}
the
nested rank
of
P
, where we note that the
value of
\sigma_{P,k}\xi_{u_{P,k}}
does not depend on the choice of
k
.

Let
T
be the tree expansion of a number
a\in\mathbf{No}
and let
v:N_{T}\longrightarrow\mathbf{No}
be the evaluation with
a=v(\rho_{T})
. An

infinite path
in
T
is a sequence
\nu_{0},\nu_{1},\ldots
of nodes
in
N_{T}
with
\nu_{0}=\rho_{T}
and
\nu_{i+1}\in C_{\nu_{i}}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Such a path induces an infinite path
P
in
a
: let
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots
be the indices with
\lambda_{\nu_{i_{k}}}=\sum
; then we take

\tau_{P,k}=v(\nu_{i_{k}+1})
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
. It is easily
verified that this induces a one-to-one correspondence between the infinite
paths in
T
and the infinite paths in
a
. We call
\xi_{\nu}:=\xi_{P}

the
nested rank
of the infinite path
\nu=(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
T
. Denoting by
I_{T}
the set of all infinite paths in
T
,
we thus have a map
\xi:I_{T}\longrightarrow\mathbf{No};\nu\longmapsto\xi_{\nu}
. We call
(T,\xi)
the
hyperserial description
of
a
.

We are now in a position to prove the final theorem of this paper.

Proof of
Theorem

1.3

Consider two numbers
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathbf{No}
with the same hyperserial
description
(T,\xi)
and let
v,v^{\prime}:N_{T}\longrightarrow\mathbf{No}

be the evaluations of
T
with
v(\rho_{T})=a
and
v^{\prime}(\rho_{T})=a^{\prime}
. We
need to prove that
a=a^{\prime}
. Assume for contradiction that
a\neq a^{\prime}
. We
define an infinite path
\nu_{0},\nu_{1},\ldots
in
T
with
v(\nu_{n})\neq v^{\prime}(\nu_{n})
for all
n
by setting
\nu_{0}:=\rho_{T}
and
\nu_{n+1}:=\nu_{n}[m]
, where
m\in\mathbb{N}
is minimal such that
v(\nu_{n}[m])\neq v^{\prime}(\nu_{n}[m])
. (Note that such a number
m
indeed exists, since
otherwise
v(\nu_{n})=v^{\prime}(\nu_{n})
using the rules

\mathbf{E1}
–
\mathbf{E7}
.) This infinite path
also induces infinite paths
P
and
P^{\prime}
in
a
and
a^{\prime}
with
a_{P,n}=v(\nu_{i_{n}})
and
a_{P^{\prime},n}=v^{\prime}(\nu_{i_{n}})
for a certain sequence
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
n>0
be such that

P_{\nearrow n}
and
P^{\prime}_{\nearrow n}
have no bad indices for
a_{P,n}

and
a_{P^{\prime},n}
. The way we chose
\nu_{0},\nu_{1},\ldots
ensures that the
coding sequences associated to the paths
P_{\nearrow n}
and
P^{\prime}_{\nearrow n}
coincide, so they induce the same nested surreal
substructure
\mathbf{Ne}
. It follows that
v(\nu_{i_{n}})=a_{P,n}=\Xi_{\mathbf{Ne}}(\sigma_{;n}\xi_{\nu})=a_{P^{\prime},n}=v^{\prime}(\nu_{i_{n}})
,
which contradicts our assumptions. We conclude that
a
and
a^{\prime}
must be
equal.

\Box
